2 McDOUGALL'S Paterit SELF—RAISING FLOUR I MEM TI ON. The Examiner, Saturday, June 10, 1905 WOMAN'S REALM.

and crossed the paik to the creek, where the " Rover " lay at her anchorage. It was a frosty night, and a full moon shone brightly in a cloudless sky. A shimmer of silver lay on the grass, and the trees sparkled with the tiozen dew. But his mind was not attuned to any beauties in the landscape. He knew that, he had acted like a weak fool, and even the fact that he was leaving Heatherstone Hall behind him for ever did not atone for the brief outburst of passion which was bound to bring sorrow into more than one life. He knew that he would always look back to those five minutes of love as a fair spot to be remem- bered,-as a shadow of a great tree in a thirsty land. Yet the sparkling well he had drunk from was not altogether sweet, and the memory of how his thirst had been quenched would always be vitiated by the bitter taste that the draught had left in his mouth. He reached the edge of the plantation, and brush- ing through the wet undergrowth, sent showers of frost flalieS from the bare branches of the trees. Then he climbed over the wall and saw the dark outline of his boat on the silvery strip of water., He stood on the bank opposite to her and hailed the man on board. After half a dozen calls James Outen came to the deck of the'l cutter. He quickly unfastened the painter and sculled to the bank. Then he waded ashore through the mud and brought his master a heavy pair of seaman's boots. Holme put them over his dress trousers and , made his way down the bank through a foot of slime. The tide was running out. "Asleep, °Uteri ? " he queried, as they pushed the boat off. "I was just dozing, sir. I'll be glad to get to my bunk." "I'm afraid you won't get there just yet," Holme said drily. "We leave here on this tide, and look sharp. We must get under weigh in ten ,Minutes, or we shan't get over the East Wick Salterns. give me one. of the sculls."

The two men bent to their work with a will. But Outen had let the dinghey run down fifty yards with the stream and every inch of this had to be fought against a slopping tide. More than half of the ten minutes had gone when they climbed up on the deck. Outen looked at his watch.

"I doubt if we do it, sir," he said. "The tide has been running out for three hours." "Confound you," said Holme, "get to work. We must get as far as we can." He had made up his mind that he would be out of sight of Heatherstone Hall when the day broke. He did not wish to set eyes on it again. The two men set to work with a will. In less than two minutes they had set the main- sail, and it began to flap in the southerly breeze. In another two minutes the tiller was in place, put hard down and pinned, and Outen was making fast the jib halliard. The jib sheets cracked backwards and forwards like writhing snakes. Then both men set to work on the anchor'. In another four minutes it came up black ,and dripping from the water." Outen held out the jib to catch the wind. Holme sprang back to the tiller, let the main sheet run out, and the boat swung round and started on her journey. For the first four miles they were running with the wind and tide, and the " Rover ' slipped past the banks like a steam launch. Holme changed his dress clothes for a blue jersey and a pair of old trousers. Then he lit his pipe and sat at the tiller, his eyes alert for every mark on the bank. He knew every inch of the channel, and crossed krom side to side of the creek to get the best water. Once only did he glance behind him, and then he saw the white walls of Heatherstone Hall shine out from a circle of trees, and the hill looming dark against the sky. In the wake of the boat the water swirled and eddied in wreaths and ripples of silver. He sighed. But for all that, he knew that the glittering path behind him only led back to dishonour, and that the dark waters before him represented the road hat he was bound to tread. The " Rover" reached the bend in the creek in twenty minutes. Outen hauled in the main sheet and set the foresail and topsail. The boat lay over on her side in a freshening breeze, and the water, which had been silent while they ran before the wind, 'began to hiss and splash and bubble at the bows. Holme looked up critically at the canvas, and gave a few orders to Outen, who began to tighten sheets and halliards. Holme liked his sails to set well, even when there was no one to see them. The moon shone on the rounded curves, and they looked like the firm plumage of at white gull. They ninved 'a trifle more slowly now, and Holnie's eyes watched the creek as a tight- rope walker watches his wire. The water was certainly running out very fast, and the channel was not more thpn thirty feet across in places. Here and there a black saltern showed its ugly little hump above the water and split the channel into two narrow rivers. Threp miles away the mound, on which the Red House stood, showed up against the flat land around it. - Holme looked at his watch. . _

"We shan't do it, Outen," he said. '`We touched just noW, and thdugh it's deeper before we get to the Red House, it's a lot shallower at East Wick. Just go for'ard and stand by the anchor.','

Outen went into the bows and kept a sharp look-out at the water ahead. When they were within a mile of the Red House, the wind began to die away, and more than once the sails flapped. However, they drifted down with the stream, and there was just enough breeze to steer by. Then suddenly Outen gave a cry of surprise. . " Put her round, sir," he yelled ; "put her round quick." • Hohne jammed down the helm, but the boat refused to come about against the tide. Outen tumbled the -anchor overboard and the chain went rattling over the bow. The sails flapped idly, but :the tide swung the boat round. Outen checked the cable and the boat brought up with a jerk. At the same moment the stern crashed into something and the whole vessel quivered with the shock. Holme looked over the stern, -but could dis- tinguish nothing in the shadow beneath the keel. _ _

"What is it ? " he cried out angrily. For answer Outen pointed to the shore on either side. Holme saw a long thin line run from the bank, disappear in the water, re- appear on the other side of the creek, and con- tinue its course to the opposite bank. It was just such an obstacle as selfish landowners on the Thames put across the backwaters which run through their territory—a stout chain hung from shore to shore. Holme uttered a savage oath and scowled at the Red House, which lay a hundred and fifty yards from them.

"I'll teach the scoundrel that the creeks don't belong ,to him," he muttered. "Can't we break clear of the cursed thing, Outen ; we shall smash our rudder."

"We can pull in a bit of cable, sir, but not much. The bottom of this creek doesn't hold very well, and the tide's running like a mill-race. We want as much chain as pos- sible." "Well,, let's pull her clear of this cursed chain. Then we'll go ashore and talk to Mr. Jordison." Holme had very good reasons for avoiding the man, but he forgot them in the heat of his anger. He .would teach this tip-, start that to buy the land on either side of the creek conferred no right to the waters that flowed between the banks. He did not realise that a meeting was exactly the object Jordi- son had in view..

They hauled in some of the anchor chain, stowed sail, and rowed ashore in the dinghey. It was now past two o'clock in the Morning, and there was no light to be seen in any part of the Red House. The two men waded up to the bank through the mud, and making their way to the door, kicked on it with their heavy boots. The noise reverberated through the house, but no one stirred • within. Holme stepped back a few paces, and looked at the cracked walls and empty window frames. He had, of course, heard of the explosion, but he knew that Jordison had not left the. neigh- bourhood. -

They walked round to the' Other side of the house and thundered, at the back door. For live minutes they kicked at the woodwork.

until they had knocked in the two bottom panels. Then suddenly a streak of yellow kght came from behind them and threw their shadows on the wall of the house. They turned round and saw that it came from an open door in the stables, and that the dark figure of a man was silhouetted against it. They left the house and walked towards hirn. As they drew near, he took the lantern from the table behind him and let it hang from his aand, so that the light fell full on tbpir faces, end his own was in darkness.

" Who are you," be cried, " and wha". the devil do you mean by making a disturbance at this time of night ? ' Moline recognised the voice, but he would Certainty have been surprised, if he could have seen the face, which was lit with a smile of triuniph, and betrayed none of the annoyance that the words implied. 'I am Arthiir Holme," was the sharp reply, and my boat is caught up on some cursed obstacle that you have put across the creek. You have got to remove it at once." "The creek is mine. The land on either side is mine."

"You are evidently a stranger to the cus- toms of these parts. Every man has a right of water-way up the creeks. You must take it down at once. Of what use is it to you ?'' "I am making a ferry from one bank to the other," Jordison replied, chuckling to himself at the fertility of his own imagination. "I want to get carts across to carry reeds from the marshes."

"You are acting against the law, Mr. Jordison, and the chain must come down." " I will risk the 4 law, but I have no wish to hinder your. boat. I will have the chain lowered."

He stepped inside the door and closed it behind him. - Holme and Outen waited out- side till he reappeared with Lipp and Jermy. . "My two setva,nts will go down with your man," said Jordison, "and loose the chain. In the meantime I want a word with you, Mr. Holme. If you will come inside the harness room, and excuse my rough hospitality, I will give you a glass of good whisky, and a really passable cigar." " I have no wish to talk to you," Holme replied. "I want to gtt under weigh at once." "You have lost the tide now. You cannot clear the East Wick Saltems. • You will have to wait at least five hours. 1 have something important to say to you, mid if you refuse to come with me, well, you certainly won't get past the chain unless you bring some more men to help you." Holme shrugged his shoulders. A few minutes conversation was ,of no importance one way or the other. He particularly wished to make the sea by daybreak. "I'll come," he said abruptly, and went inside with Jordison. _ _ _

The other three men disappeared round the corner of the house. Jordison poured out two glasses of whisky, and offering a cigar to his guest, lit one himself. "Now, then, Mr. Holme," he said, looking the young man straight in the face, " I am going to ask you a question. I asked it you before, sand you departed somewhat uncere- moniously without answering it. This time it will take a more precise-form. Tell me what you know of the death of Richard Behag." Holme was silent. He had expected this question, and had already resolved to give no answer. But he did not expect what was to follow.

"Tell me what you know of the death of Richard Behag," Jordisbn repeated slowly, "and tell me whether you know anything oi Arthur Sterious, who is said to have killed him." Holme half rose from his chair, and his face grew white. But he recovered himself. "You seem to know all about it, Mr. Jon dison," he replied. "I can't , tell you more than what is common gossip in Valparaiso. Why are you so interested in the story? ' . .

Jordison looked at the speaker from undez his shaggy eyebrows. " I was a great friend of, Sterious's father," he said. "He has written to me, and asked me to search for his son in England. New facts have come to light which have proved Sterious to be innocent. Can you tell me anything about him ? " Holme .the story was a .trifle weak.

"Why should i know aiwthing ? ' "Arthur Sterious is in this neighbourhood," Jordison answered, and watched the effect 01 this well-aimed shot.

" Really I I don't know the gentleman." _

For answer Jordison pulle-cl out a gold watch and flung it on the table. Holme glanced 4tt it, and rose to his feet. But he controlled his agitation. "Very interesting," he said. "May I ask where you found it ? " "In one of the gulleys in the marshes. Now do you know anything of Arthur Sterious i "

fiolme was silent. - " You refuse to give me the information ? " " I do refuse." "I must have it." Holme laughed. " How do you propose to get it ? " he asked.

Jordison took the revolver from his pocket, and Icoking at it, glanced at Holme and smiled.

..... "DO you intend to shoot me ? " the young man asked.

"Not I. Dead men cannot speak, and I want you to speak. But you do not leave here until you tell me what I want to know." "I can tell you nothing." Jordison went to the door, and taking a whistle from his pocket, blew three shrill calls upon it. A few seconds later Holme heard cries, and the splashing of water. Then there was 'a wild shriek for help and then silence. He sprang at Jordison, who blocked up the doorway, but the latter moved aside. Holme tripped and came with a crash to the ground. The next moment the two men were locked in each other's arms. In a minute Lipp and jermy came running up, and the three soon overpowered Holme. He was gagged, bound hand and foot, and carried to the Red House. Lipp was set to watch by aim till the morning:

"I think you will speak, Mr. Holme, before I have done with you," said Jordison, as he eft the room.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

CARD-LEAVING.

Card-leaving is a subject of supreme importance during the earlier weeks of a London season, says ILA. P. One of the most marked changes in our social life is that actual calls become fewer, while the business of cards increases year by year. An arrival in town must be announced by leaving cards in person, sending them by a servant, or having them sent round by a shop. The smart set have altered the fashion of card-leaving. In old days a married woman calling on another married' couple left one of her own cards and two of her husband's. Now she leaves one of hers and only one of his. Then, in former years, a married woman who called on another matron, and found her at home, used to leave two of her, husband's cards on the hall-table when she left the house after paying her visit. But this is no longer the custom. Indeed, some ultra-smart married women never Pave any cards ^of their husbands at all, except when calling for the first time on a new acquaintance.

An afternoon gown of blue and white checked voile is shewn in the sketch. This material being so light and fine in texture, it is as well to have the skirts made very full. SERVANTS' REGISTRIES.

The powers that be have at last taken the matter af servants' registries into their potent, grave, learned, but very slow-going consideration; and it was quite time, says the. Sketch. The London County Council, in seeking powers of control over employment-agencies, domestic and otherwise, is not a day too soon in its hope of stopping the fraudulent practices frequently resorted to by certain of the lower class of servants' registry offices. As Lord Robert Cecil pointed out in the House, unhappy girls are defrauded of their shilling or halt-crown on the promise of a situation which is rarely or never obtained, and have no redress whatever for the loss of their money. But it is the mistress who is more frequently victimised, and on whom these disreputable agencies really exist. She enters; she asks for the required sort of servant; she is assured many are on the books and will be immediately forthcoming. She is cajoled into paying a fee, or a preliminary half-fee even, and she is forthwith at the mercy of these sharks, who, to keep within the law, send one or two impossible specimens, and after that promises. Naturally, one exempts the .well-con- ducted offices, often run by women of good birth nowadays, which are obviously safe ,and satis- factory. But these are not all, and dozens of dishonest dens abound where mistress and maid alike are fleeced and defrauded. Mo RN MG COSTIIIIE.

Our sketch shews a morning costume in light brown Sicilian. The little coat is quite plain, and has a roll-back collar of brown velvet overlaid with shaped pieces of ivory cloth, adorned with fancy buttons. The sleeves are leg-of-mutton in shape, and terminate in narrow cuffs which exactly match the collar. The skirt is plain, wide at the hem, and long, having as sole trimming two bands of

brown velvet appliqué with ivory cloth in the same manner as the coat collar. A plain tucked shirt of creamy lawn is worn under the coat, finished by a turn-down collar and brown silk stock. The hat /which accompanies this costume is of ivory satin Straw, bound and trimmed with brown velvet, and baying a large cache-pique of creamy loses and foliage. SUN-PROOF CLOTH.

A real novelty in the sartorial world, says the Tatierdis the new sun-proof cloth, which is making something of a seisation, the Prince and Princess of Wales having orderad sundry wrap coats and wraps Of it far their Indian tour. Its great advantage is, of course, that it neither fades nor burns, and is the most comfortawile thing for wearing in the tropics yet discovered. CAPILOTADE OF WILD DUCK.—lrry a dessert- spoonful of chopped onion with two tablespoon- fuls of salad oil in a stewpan until it is a golden colour; add the neatly trimmed pieces of cold, rather underdone wild duck, and turn them about for a few minutes in the oil ; then pour in half a tumbler of claret; add a tablespoon- ful of red-currant jelly, and cover the stewpan until the pieces of duck are quite hot, without boiling; then add a pint of good brown sauce, which should be made with the stock from the boiled bones of the bird. THE BLACK MOTOR CAR.

By J. B. HARRIS BURLANA

CHAPTER XV.—Continueb

It was all very sudden, and very um maidenly, but the look 'on his face compelled it. For the moment her redon had left her, and she had acted as her heart commanded. But a second later, she broke away from his clasp, and burying her face in her, hands, cried as though her heart would break. He came to her side again. ," Agnes," he said, softly, "I implore you, dear Agnes—oh God, what have I done ? Don't cry, dearest; don't cry like that." Again he took her in his arms, and this time she did not try to free herself from his em- brace. But she looked up at him with plead- ing eyes. He bent down his face and kissed her tenderly on the forehead. Hi* whole nature cried out for the touch of her lips, but he was man enough to subdue the passior that burnt within him.

She looked into his face again, and he say* that glad light of love shine through the tears in her eyes. But still she did not speak. "Agnes,. dearest," he said in a low voice, "I have something to say to you, and I must say it quickly. I would have left without saving it, !At it must be said now—after what has happened." She turned her eyes away from his-face, and he drew her closer to him. He could feel her slim body tremble in his arms. "Agnes," he said simply, "I love you. It sounds weak to say you are the only woman I have ever loved. It is a stock phrase ; the ridicule of cynics ; in most cases a lie, that no intelligent woman believes. But in my case it is the real truth. Yet to-night I must leave you, and never see you again. I wilt not drag you down to my level. But for a moment of weakness, but for the prank that chance has played us, I should have left without telling you this. New that I 4 have told it, I must go." "Arthur," she murmured, but so faintly that the sound hardly came to his ears. It was the first word she had spoken, and all the love of her heart was concentrated in the opftly breathed name. An appeal, a despair- ing cry, a prayer for him to stay. It was all these blended together by love in a single word.

"Yes, I must leave you: Agnes," he con- tinued, "I am no fit husband for you. It is not only that you are a great lady, and I am a poor man. Were I but the son of a farmei on your father's estate, I could look you honestly in the face and ask you to be my wife. But being what I am, I could not drag you down to rriy level. I see you for the last time, Agnes. I must go now."

"Yes, I think it is .-time that Mr. Arthur Holme went," said a stern cold voice behind them.

They, sprang apart and turning round con- fronted' the Earl of Hpatherstone. His heavy ' handsome face was flushed with anger, but he controlled himself with the skill of the trained I diplomat. Lady Agnes hid her crimson face ' in her hands. Arthur Holme clenched right fist, and ,said a word that would not look well in print. "Mr. Arthur Holme is going, Lord Heather- ' stone," he said. "How long have you been here." He snapped the last half dozen works out so savagely • that Lord Heatherstone shrank back a. pace. But he recovered his composure in a moment.

"Agnes," he said quietly, "go up to your bedroom at once. Mr. Holme,. I will have a word with you before you go." Lady Agnes went up to her infuriated father and kissed him meekly on the cheek. "Good night,!' she said in a low voice. He did not answer, but turned his back on her. She held out her hand timidly to Arthur Holme.

" Good-bye," she said in a whisper. Holme looked at her father, and the devil rose in his heart. He stepped forward to clasp her in his arms and kiss her passionately on the lips. But his finer nature prevailed. He Stopped suddenly and held out his hand. "Good-bye," he said hoarsely, "and God bless you."

---S-h-ejt-oo—k his hand, rooked for a moment into his eyes, and fled. He turned to the great Earl of Heatherstone.

"Well, my lord," he said sharply, "what have you to say for yourself. It was characteristic of the man that he assumed the attitude of a judge, at a time when his proper place was in the dock.

" It is for you to speak, Mr. Holme," Lord Heatherstone replied ; "but let us return to the smoking-room. It is cold in here."

The two- men went back to the smoking- room. Lord Heatherstone flung himself in an easy chair by the fire. Holme remained standing. For a while neither of them spoke. "Well, sir," said Lord Heatherstone, after a pause. _ - -

." As you have been listening, my lord, I think you know everything. It is possibly clear to you that I love your daughter. It is equally clear, if you heard rightly, that I shall not see her again." _ _

"You damned scoundrel ! " cried Lord Heatherstone, rising to his feet, "what right have you to speak of love to my daughter! Who are you, you damned adventurer? Don't you know your place, confound you? Do you think that because we—" "That'll do, Lord Heatherstone," the young man replied; "I know my place quite well. And it is because I know it that I have resolved never to enter this house again. I will wish you good-bye, my lord."

- " Stay, Mr. Holnie'; 3-rou cannot go yet. How long has this been going on? " "It commenced five minutes before you came into the room. It ended when your daughter left it. But I shall answer no more questions. I wish you good night," and he turned to go. Lord Heatherstone laughed. " When I was your age," he said in an insolent tone, "I had more pluck. I would not have broken the heart of the woman I loved for ,au the social distinctions in the world. Bah I I thought there was more tight in you. I do not think Agnes has lost much." •

Arthur Holme turned sharply round, and his face paled for all the bronze that the sea and _winds had given it.

"There's prenty of fight in me, Lord Heatherstone, he replied in a strained voice. " And, by God, if you rouse it, you will bring the devil into your household. lam man enough to retire without wrecking your home. Even if I thought that your own case had made you lenient to us both, even if I thought that your second marriage with a woman whom you dragged up from obscurity had made you less punctilious about the alliances of your house, I would not offer myself as a suitor for your daughter's hand." You have no money," said Lord Heather- stone. "It would, of course, be an impossible match. We do not even know who you are." "I know this," Holme replied gravely, A' that I am no fit husband for any woman. But, mark you. Lord Heatherstone, if you rouse the devil in me, I may cast all scruples to the winds. I am a strong man, and I care little far your lands and your titles. If I did resolve to follow my desires, Lord Heather- st9ne, I would indeed 'leave your house unto ydu desolate.'" Lord Heatherstone sank into a chair and buried his face in his hands. Arthur Holme had struck hard, and struck deep. It was but a month since Lord Overcliffe had died.

Holme W. 3 disgusted with the brutality of his own words. He stepped over to Lord Heatherstone and laid one hand on his shoulder. '

" jam sorry, Lord, Heatherstone," he said quietly ;" I did not mean to hurt you. You would forgive me much if you knew what this brief glimpse of love has been to me. • Good night ancl—good-bye."

BLACKBURN'S QUEEN VICTORIA STATUE.

Final arrangements were made on Wednesday for the unveiling of the Queen Victoria Memorial Statue in Blackburn, by the Princess Louise (Duchess of Argyll.) A portion of the site has been given by the vicar of the Parish Church, and the statue will be composed of white marble on a grey granite base. Standing lift. in height, the memorial will be a dignified and regal one. The robing is finely designed. Surrounding the site will be an ornamental balustrade of Yorkshire stone. The sculptor is Mr. Bertram Mackennal.

I CommtnclAE FAlLllRES.—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the total number of Commercial , Failures recorded in England and Wales during the , week ending Friday, June 2nd, was 163, viz. : —New bankruptcy proceedings published in the London Gazette, 84; deeds of arrangement registered, 79. The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were: Bankruptcies, 89; deeds of arrangement 56—total 145; being an increase of 18. The toinl number of commercial failures recorded during the 22 weeks of the present year is 3,954 ; the total number recorded in the corresponding 22 weeks of last year was 3,829 shcwing an increase of 125.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Lady Londonderrv.—The Marchioness of London- derry, who gave a brilliant reception in honour of the King of Spain, is at present the leading hostess on the Unionist side, as Lady Lansdowne is not attending Court, or entertaining this season, in consequence of the recent death of her mother—the Dowages Duchess of Abercorn. It has been said that the influence of women in politics died with Lady Palmerston, but this is scarcely correct, and the Marchioness of Londonderry has not only entertained a great deal for her party, but has always been of the greatest assistance to her husband in his political career. She also finds much to employ her in philanthropic and charitable undertakings, and, at Wynyard Park, the family seat On the borders of Dur- ham and Yorkshire, the Marchioness takes an active part in the management of the Home Farm and the gardens are her especial care. She has also done rnech good work in the colliery districts of Durham, where she has established schools and nursing institutions. Londc.n Gaieties.—Year by year. the aristocracy appear to he getting more tired of the monotonous round of balls, dinners, concerts, and receptions, which constitute the London season, and many of the leading I people in the world of fashion are taking advantage of the Whitsuntide recess, to get away to- the country. They are beginning to discover that in May and June, the country is at its best, and that there is no law which compels them to follow the custom of their parents, and grandparents, who always spent May, June, and July in London and kept open house there for the entertain- ment of their friends and relatives. The motor car has done much to substitute flying visits for settled periods of residence, whilst the development of hotel and restaurant life, has, to a larger extent dispensed with private entertaining. Some people speak of these changes as if they meant the decadence of Society, but broadly speaking all they mean is that people of wealth and fashion spend a lesson part of the finest months in the year in London, than was once thei custom. The Royal visit has crowded London to its utmost, but with the departure of the King of Spain the season will begin to wane, for, after the Royal wedding next week, the principal social events will be Ascot, Henley, Good- wood, and Cowes, and then the fashionable world goes' to Scotian.", and to foreign Spas, in search of " cures.' The Safe Keeping of Jewels.—Great jewel robberies in which the leading noble families are usually the victims, recur as regularly as the seasons come round, but it is unfortunate that there should have been such an occurrence at Grosvenor House, the town residence of the Duke and Duchess of Westminster. It is the more regrettable because Grosvenor House is frequently thrown open to the public for philanthropic and charit- able purposes, and the theft of the Duchess's jewels will inevitably make the Duke, and also other owners more chary of lending their mansions for such under- takings. Another effect of these robberies seems to be to increase the number of orders to jewellers for false and duplicate sets of jewels, which are made to serve the purpose of the real ones, whilst the latter remain safely deposited with the bankers. The satisfaction of the possession of jewels comes very nearly akin to the joy and pride of wearing them, but otherwise, if imita-' tions are to be made to do, it would be an advantage to dispose of the real ones, and avoid the anxiety which their safe keeping necessarily involves. Toilettes at the Races.—Dreas at Epson, owing to the perfect weather, closely resembled that usually in evidence at Ascot, and it is to be feared that many wearers of toilettes only suitable fog the latter festivity are regretting having subjected their beautiful "creations "to this somewhat trying ordeal. White was the general wear, chiffon, silk muslin and brocierie Anglaise, being the fabrics preferred for frocks of the airy-fairly per -uasion. Hop sack, herring-bone serge, and fine cloths, were in favour for the tailor-mades, the trimming consisting of pastel-shaded embroidery, a gown of broderie Anglaise is an enviable possession.bnt nothing second rate in this direction, can be tolerated for a moment. Race Wrap's.—The greater number of race coats this season are built in Empire style, a mode which possesses many advantages. First and foremost, this particular wrap is easy to slip on and off, an important item at a race meeting; it gives height to the woman whose inches are too few; and again, it can be fashioned as elaborately, or as simply as one pleases, and look well either way. White linen covered with eyelet embroidery is having a great vogue for the more expensive race coats, and this is sometimes made up over a very pale silk lining, though a white lining is perhaps in better taste. SanishModes.—Whether the high tortoiseshell comb and the mantilla, have come to stay remains to be seen, but at the theatre, and at the opera, they have been donned daring the past week by several leading women in society. The bright r d rose, tucked behind the left ear too, is another Spanish mode that is being closely followed, especially by brunettes.—And further, milliners have been trying their best, to introduce the toreador bat, but we have only just recovered from the "pork pie " epidemic, and, save by a few extremists, the famous round Spanish hat, with its trimming of pom-poms, is being left severely alone. At the moment the Louis xxi shape is much in evidence, its straight flat brim being set far forward over the eyes. Bead Necklaces Fashionable.—No afternoon costume is deemed complete without a necklace of beads, which repeats the accessory colour. For example, with a creamy white gown, and a white hat trimmed with vivid green, a necklace of chrysoprase, also in the same tone of green would be donned, while if purple should be the relieving colour chosen, amethyst beads would be decided upon. All jewellers, and large drapery emporiums are stocking these coloured beads, great bowls, full of every possible colour and variety, being presented to our admiring gaze. The Cat Modish.—The fashionable woman who fails to possess a do of some description, is a rarity in these days, and latelg y it has become quite" the thins' "to invest in some curiosity in the form of a cat. With the exception of Princess Victoria of Schlesurg- Holstein, who has frequently exhibited her pets, cats have found little favour with members of our Royal Family, and this perhaps has had much to do with pussey's social banishment. Choice specimens hailing from Siam, India, Thibet, Kashmir, Russia, Persia, etc., are now however to be met with "in Society," one hailing from the latter country being valued at the record price of 700 guineas. The Ladies Golf Charripionship.—Beautiful weather favored the tournament for the ladies golf champion- ship at Cromer, in which Miss Thompson,of the Beverley and East Riding Club,was finally the victor. The links at Cromer are known far and wide amongst golfers, for their sporting and interesting features, and some excel- lent play was seen during the contest. Additional interest in the match was afforded by the entry of several lady players from the other side of the Atlantic including Miss G. Rishop,the lady champion of America —who is a practised and extremely strong match player. All the visitors however were defeated before the final tie, in which Miss Thompson who has been a regular competitor for the championship for several years, was the victor by 3 up, and 2to play, over Miss E. Stuart of the Royal Portrush Club. Both players went out in 23 strokes, and turned at the ninth hole all square but in the return Miss Stuart seems to have had the worst of the luck, and this often means a great deal to the game when played on a course which presents so many hazards as is the case at Cromer.

CURIOUS FINDS IN QUEER PLACES.

FELIX ELIOT (Ant.lior of " Romance of Gold .Digging," It c.)

I once was very near making a small fortnne, but I resisted the temptation .I did not accept the offer of a Cape-boy, who at the time, acted us a sort of gyp in my two-roomed, iron-covered shanty. I was just leaving for office, when Wilson, the gyp in question, in a. mysterious whisper asked if he might " show me somelin." In response to my affirmative he produced from his pocket a small packet, the size ,of which be- came considerably reduced before the " some- thing" was exposed. It proved to be a dimnond of course, seeing that the locals was Kimberley, South Africa—a- diamond a con- siderable Sire, very neariy as big •as a pigeon- egg. • . .

I was somewhat startlyd at the sight, but soon recovered my equanimity. " Well, Wilson, what have you there P' • " Di' mond, I.3ans." , " Yes, so I see; Why bring it to me ?" " I thought Bails would like to have it." " Did you? Where did you get it ?" " Picked it up, Bass—off the floor." • It was no use asking what floor, so I merely added, " You want to sell it ?" •

A leer of satisfaction spread over Wilson's face.

" Ja, Baas. How much 'Master give ?" " What do you.wau t for it ?"

Master will give me ton pounds ?" I took the stone from him, examined it well, thought of the possibilities, and of one or two probabaikes, then with a, sigh of regret, handed the stone back to • Wilson with the recommenda- tion to him to restore it to the place from whence he took it. Of course, I know now, exactly, how big a fool I was. Also I know now what the stone was worth. How Clod know ?

A 811CCF.SSITL SNItiGOT.ER.

Listen!! A fortnight after the event, a man I knew slightly, a diamond-broker,. and a suc- cessful one, too, told, me, he was going home. ile.was a colonial born youth, but home meant, in this case, England. I wished him a pleasant time, and in return he . showed me a sample of fine diamonds Which he was taking to London on behalf of the firms be represented. I bad no reason to disbelieve him. His sample was a splendid one, indeed. Ile was on the point of leaving me, when be said, " Look here, I do not show this to every fellow I know, but come in.bere."

. , In the retired nook to which we adjourned, he discovered to my view the very stone my gyp had offered me a week or ten days before. " A line stone," I remarked. " Where did you get it F" " Oh, I bought it as a spec." Then I remembered that Wilson also acted as gyp to other omen, of nhom the possessor of the diamond was one.

. He went home, stayed some six months, and had a good time, as he admitted to me on his return—" a very good time "—with emphasis. " Did you sell your parcel satisfactorily ?" " 0 yes, I may say my ,first visit to Hattor Garden was a decided success." "

" And the large stone—did you :manage to get rid of it?" " Large stone !" he repeated, looking some- what paler in line. " What larger stone ?" " Don't you remember showing me the stone you bought fr um old Wilson. It was old Wilson wasn't it t"

I enjoyed his confusion, but perhaps felt a little envious of his luck.

He has forgotten all about that period now- a-days. He .is . one of , South Africa's million- aires : drives first-rate cattle, and envelopes him- self in 'costly furs when our boreal friend Jack Frost seizes upon his Austral-Africe skin.

A DIAMOND AND A DINNER.

A relative of mine, digging on the. Vaal River for diamonds had a run of ill-luck that left •him financially stranded at the end of some months of labour. He was " stoney-broke." in fact. His liabilities at the Store gave him 'uneasiness. His larder wild very nearly in the state of Old Mother Hubbard's at the time she felt charitably disposed to her dog. He was hungry for flesh. Bud, beast Or fish, it mattered, little to him which. He borrowed a gun—having hypothecated his own some 'weeks previously. He sallied forth into the bush, and shot a -monster paarav (a bird of the bastard family), took it to his hut, and prepared it for eating on the succeeding day. He thought giblet- soup not a bad beginning to a meal. When preparing these morsels for the stew-pot, he,:of course, opened up the gizzard, Wherein, amongst other things, he 'found it small finger-ring of 9-carat gold and an uncut diamond of some "fourteen carets, hot by any means it bad stone, seeing that ,he sold it later to it well-known buyer for it little less than £BOO. Moral : being, hungry the • shooting of bustards which have appetites for such dainties as 14-carat diamonds is simpler work than digging. What do you think of the artfulness •of the contrabandist who evaded the detectives' search by cutting off the tip of a bullock's horn, hol- lowing out the thicker portion that remained on the head, inserting his Si carat stone and fixing the tip of the horn on again, and by smearing it with mud put the " tees " off the scent. Simple and novel, eh ? Another individual sought to evade the officers by boring an auger hole in the dissel-boiim or pole of s W. 57011. It didn't come off. The detectives utilised a saw in cutting that dissel-boom into sections, burn them, and finally discovered the precious gem.

A DIAMOND-FILLED TOOTH.

- Perhaps the queerest place in which a diamond has ever been found was in the mouth of a hippopotamus. This find was in the cemented gravels through which the Vaal River has worn its way. These gravels are a much earlier denos;t than those which have becn worked from the time of the first discovery of diamonds in South Africa. . The digger who had the luck to find this queer speennen was prospecting in the bed of the river at a spot where he bad ingeniously dammed off the waters. Ire came first of all upon the ribs of the beast, then the fore-quarters later upon the bead, it perfect skeleton. The interior was filled with cemented gravel, but he broke off one of the jaws ancl while examining it became aware that one of the teeth had de- cayed. In this hollow he found embedded among a cement of lime and sand, a diamond of some 1 carats. The stone was a good one. Fortunately he did not disturb it in its setting. Later on he sold it to a buyer who gave him a price more than six times the value of the stone. :Even diamond-buyers . can find time to indulge in a love for curios.

At this period when " illicit diamond buy- ing" was rife, the writer was engaged in digging for the precious .crystals among the gravels on the banks of the Vaal River. Luck nt the time was nowhere very much in the e.scendant, and when a stranger arrived—or., too, looking very 'mused to toil of any description, to ask where he might put up his pegs, curicsity to a certain extent became aroused. The newcomer having established the necessary beacons for marking out his elfim, departed, ot,!y to return again some half-hour later armed with a brand-new pick and shovel. ,He at orce set to work though garbed very unlike any ot his neighbours, a stiff white linen collar being a quaint symbol of .the digging profession. However, he appeared to be trorkiny. Scarce hal fan hour sufficed for his purpose. A loud shout reached a neighbour who, on locking up, saw the would-be digger gesticulating wildly and beckoning. The ordinary digger does not respond to every casual beckoner's desire; It 'happened so in. .this case. Whereupon the gestienlator scrambled madly over the intervening heaps of de;hris, and at last reached the our whose attention he sought iv breathless baste, said :

POINT IN BANKRUPTCY LAW.

Sequel to a Warrington Failure ?

A meeting was held on Tuesday at the offices of the Manchester Official Receiver (Mr. J. Grant Gibson) of the creditors of T. Lomas Cross, of 34, West-street, Warrington. and Walter Cross, of 10, West-street, War- rington, carrying on business at Longford-street, Warrington, as builders, under the style of T. and W. Cross.

The debtors were declared bankrupt in 1896, and the ineeting had now been called to consider the offer made by the mortgages and purchasers of certain real estate acquired by Walter Cross since the bankruptcy, to pay such a sum as would produce, with the dividend already paid, 12s. 6d. in the £ on all provable debts in the bank- ruptcy, with the fees and costs of the Board of Trade. A dividend of lid. in the £ had previously been paid on liabilities amounting to £],B2. The aggregate amount of the unsecured liabilities was £2,159. The Official Receiver pointed out that While it was probable that the trustee in the first bankruptcy was entitled to realise real property acquired by an undis- charged bankrupt to the extent required to discharge the debts of the first bankruptcy in full, in the absence of a decision by the House of Lords, it was not certain that such was tile case. Walter Cross had erected a considerable ambunt of house property on freehold land since the date of his first bankruptcy. The Chairman of the Warrington Slate Company moved as an amendment that 15s. in the £ be accepted. and the meeting agreed to the suggestion that thep Official Receiver should place the matter before the mortgages and purchasers.

HE HAD A FEW QUARTS.

"Beer in camp is issued very siaringly," writes a con- tributor to the "Windsor." 'The canteens are only open for its sale during certain hours, and no man is allowed to purchase more than a couple of pints a day. This is generally found to be enough; some soldiers, however, seem to have an extraordinary capacity for drinking, or else manoeuvring is provocative of extreme thirst, as they have no difficulty in disposing of six. times the regulation allowance. A story is told of a private who, patronising the canteen too freely, was brought up the next morning for drunkenness. 'Now Murphy,' said the Colonel, 'tell me the truthi and let you off lightly. Were you drunk yesterday?" 'Certainly not, sorr,' was the indignant reply. '()h, come,' returned the other, 'you don't mean to tell me you didn't drink any beer at nil?' 'Well, your Honour,' answered Murphy candidly, wasn't what you would call squiffy, but) don't mmdadmitting I did have a few quarts.'

Mr DEAR CHILDREN,

The letters that I have received from -the members of the various branches of the Children's Corner League have delighted me. I had no idea that such, interest would be taken by my little friendis in the idea of a corner all to themeelves.

The more lettere I receive the better I am pleased. Ido not mind how long it takes me to read through your friendly communications; you can rest assured that my heart is always with you in all. your doings. A day or two since I received a letter from a little girl of eight, telling me all about an addition to her house in the,,shape of five little Persian kittens. I was greatly pleased with this letter, as I am a great lover of cats. Every- 4one of our Boys and Girls should have some pet, if it is only a garden snail; even these in- *ects have been known to develop extra- rdinary instincts and have become quite friendly in their curious way to the person who has fed them. THE FLABBERGASTER. (A Tale for Children.)

His Majesty of 801-80 l Was miserable. So was her Majesty; likewise the Royal Courtiers; also the Royal menials; in fact, everybody in 80l- 80l had just got as bad a dose of the miserables as could possibly be. For the past month a cloud of ennui seemed to have settled upon the Royal tiles, and, as said before, everybody was thoroughly touched with the blue hump. The King could stand it Ono longer; neither could the Queen; nor the Courtiers, or even the Royal scullions, so at a meeting convened by the Lord Chamberlain to see what could be done, the Lord Chief Money-Spender suggested the advertising for a Court Jester at a salary of about £20,000 a year, including washing. The King shrieked his assent, and everybody mar- velled how it was that the Court could have done without a Jester so long. In due time, the advertisement appeared in the "Court News," and in due time so many men applied for the Royal situation, all bringing yearns of testi- monials 'to give them an extra push—with the exception of one man with so long and melan- choly a face that it made him look as miserable as all thereat of the Court put together. After reading the recommendations of the others, his Majesty beckoned to him of the long face, who forthwith prostrated himself upon the Royal Kidderminster. "Menial," proceeded the King, " what hest thou for thy recommendation r " My Liege," cried the man, "I have only been guilty of one funny tale. which I told to my mother-in-law, whereupon she was so tickled that she went off into a shrieking fit and never recovered." The King expressed a desire to hear the story, and, gathering up his ermine gown, knelt down beside the man, and had it whis- pered into his Royal ear. "Capital," cried the King; "Great!" (screamed the Royal parrot; and the man was forthwith invested with the title of the Royal Court Jester, with instructions to take up his duties the following day, and to open the ball kind of business with a demon- stration of his entertaining powers. The following day came, and with it one of the greatest and most gcitgeous assemblies 80l- 80l had ever witnessed. Everybody appeared in new gowns an dresses\ and the Lord Cham- berlain rin his new suit which came home at Easter. The Court trumpeters blew a Royal fan- fare fl.o the approach of their Majesties, and then everybody, after a little preliminary bow- ing and scraping, settled down and awaited the dispersion of "lee miserables." At a signal from the King, the Court Jester stepped out into the limelight, and, after thanking their Majesties for his appointment and assuring them of his strict attention to business at all times, pro- ceeded to work up to the great finale. The Court was hushed, and every nerve at a high tension as the C.J. propagated the following extra- ordinary riddle : • "Why is a mouse when it spins?" (To be continued next week.) Once on a time a Cupid, who For learning had a bent, Insisted that young pansies should To some good school be sent. He sat him on a spider's web, • And said, with knowing wink, "Since you young ,pansies stand for \' thoughts,' You must be taught to think."

As to the cost, the figures were given to him by the officials, and he certainly Let us assume that the driver has become unconscious should not be a party to any juggling, with dramatic suddenness. We must also assume that Upon the amendment being put to the meeting it there is a passenger to his left, for if be bad only one was lost, companion he would probably be in the front seat. From his position he could not conveniently reach the hand-brake. The clutch-pedal, however, would be quite accessible, and possibly he could apply the brake-pedal also. Absolutely the quickest way, however, of bring- ing a car to a standstill is to turn off the electric current and stop the engine instantaneously; not only, in, fact, is the engine stopped, but it would then itself act as a powerful brake upon the car. The switch is usually placed upon the dash-board—not invariably within easy reach of the driver himself, though oftener than not, but certainly always in a position which is accessible to the passenger. Any person, therefore,who is apprehensive on the subject should ascertain, when he in ounts a ca r, where the switch is placed,—"Country Life"

AN ATHLETE'S PREDICAMENT.

Scrofula from Infected Rubbing Gloves

Zam-Buk Cures.

The danger of catching skin disease, and the never- failing curative agency to be found in Zam-Buk, will be forced on one's mind by reading the experience of• Mr. H. Colledge, of 166,1 Eagle-street, Coventry, who is well-known as an athlete in the Midlands. To the "Coventry Reporter" representative, Mr.Colledge said: "While at some sports I lent a young fellow my rubbing gloves, and although he had some pimples on his body I did not apprehend any ill-effects. A few days afterwards, however, pimples appeared oh my right leg. They grew worse, multiplied alarmingly, and matter began to ooze from them. The disease, which resembled a form of scrofula, spread to both legs, and great pain and inconvenience attended it. I consulted doctors and chemists, spending pounds on them, and used many bottles of medicine, five sorts of ordinary ointment, and four kinds of power. The disease did not abate, and it seemed as if nothing could conquer it. After being under doctors' treatment for nine months, a friend told me to ,trtr Zam-Buk. I was very sceptical after my experience, but I purchased a box and applied it with a piece of linen night and morning. After I had used one box the scabs began to disappear, leaving holes which Zam-Buk rapidly filled with new, healthy flesh. After completing the second box the disease 'had almost gone. I con- tinued the treatment with a third box., and my legs are now quite sound. There is no indication of the disease except the scars, which will, of course, disappear in time. r feel deeply grateful for the wonderful effects of Zam-Buk. I have since recommended the balm to a friend who was similarly troubled, and he also has been cured." Zam-Buk has proved invaluable for catching skin- diseases, spreading scabs, running sores, scrofula, blisters, chafing, eczema, itch, cramp, stiff joints, raw chins, blackheads, heat rash, abscesses, pimples, ulcers, festering sores, sore feet, weak ankles, ringworm, sprains, strains, stiffness, bad legs, piles, swollen knees, inflamed patches, boils, scalp irritation, poisoned wounds, dog bites, cat scratches, rheumatism, neuralgia. sciatica. Price, Is. lid. pert box, or 2s. 9d. for family size (2s. 9d. box contains nearly four times the is. lid). Of all chemists, or post'free for same price, from the Zam- Buk Co., 4, Red Cross Street, E.C.

Mr. BALFOUR THE TWO PROPHETS OF repeated on Satur- PROTECTION.—MR. day the admission CHAMBERLAIN'S he was good enough SPEECH. to make at the Albert Hall that the country is against him. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN made the same admission at St. Helens. The evidence is too strong to be resisted any longer. Chichester is not so good as Whitby for the Oppositiou, but the reduction of the 'rory majority by 1,400 is nearly as significant as defeat. Accepting this, Mr. BALFOUR tells us airily that the inevitable Liberal Ministry is " pre- destined to fill merely the position of a stop- gap." Mr. CHAMBERLAIN takes the same view, and puts it with the greater emphasis of his florid rhetoric, "I am perfectly certain that in a few months' experience of them', the country will be much more anxious to get rid of them than it ever was to put them in." It is a pity that Mr. CHAMBERLAIN cannot persuade Mr. BALFOUR to hasten the fulfilment of this prediction. If the Liberal Ministry is to sicken the country in a few ' months, why not letit come in without any further delay? Mr. BALFOUR kindly admits that the Liberals did :some good about seventy years ago. But for the last forty years at any rate it is the principles of his own party which have con- ferred inestimable boons on the nation. The nation it serves is a little fatigued by its welfare under this beneficent dispensation, and wants to try the other. But it will soon regret this aberration, says Mr. BALFOUR, and then the virtues of Unionists will resume their sway. All this is an excellent argument for a speedy Dissolu- tion. Why not let the country have what Mr. BALFOUR describes as a Radical debauch, and get it over? The tenacity with which he sticks to office, however, suggests a misgiving in his mind that when he loses it he will not return quite so soon as he would have us believe.

Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S speech was remarkable chiefly for the loud assertion that his policy has been adopted by the Prime Minister. Those Unionists who believed that Mr. Be.r.Fouß had dissociated himself from Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN'S Colonial Preference, and had rescued his party from Protection by the safa and nebulous formula of Retaliation, must be rather concerned to find Mr. CHAMBERLAIN heartily subscribing to the official programme. Colonial Preference, he says, is now acknowledged by the Prime Minister as the first item of Unionist policy. This means, of course, a duty on corn—that is to say the taxa- tion of food. And yet Mr. BALFOUR has told us that he will never be a party to the taxation of food. Is this pledge to go about the Colonial Conference ? Ia this, then, the outcome of Mr. BALFOUR'S negotiations with Mr. CHAMBER. LAIN P Or is that wily tactician putting his own construction on the situation so as to force Mr. BALrotrit's hand ? That is a question for the Unionist party and not for us. As Mr. CHAMBERLAIN justly remarks, the Liberal party are flatly opposed to the whole business. They will not have a duty on corn, and they do not believe that it can be so juggled with as not to be felt by the " poorest of our people." Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, who allowed himself unbounded freedom of assertion at St. Helens, holds that his policy is "a constructive one," and that the policy of the Radicals is " exhausted when they have sat themselves down in their armchairs." On

the contrary, it is in his armchair' that Mr CHAMBERLAIN has been vainly striving to con- struct a scientific tariff. The experience of many years has constructed our fiscal system, which the country sees no reason to change. No duty on corn, it is plain, will satisfy our Protectionists. The whole of the St. Helens speech breathes a desire for a tariff corresponding to the tariffs which in Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S Opinion have con- ferred priceless blessings on Germany and the United States. We know those blessings, and have not the smallest wish to share them.

"Lord! how it talked!" one might say of the Council on Tuesday. The meeting was as near to creating a record for length as its predecessor was for brevity. Four solid hours were spent in some very free discussion, and at ten o'clock, when members were marching out in groups, the Mayor summarily closed tne proceedings with one or two matters on the agenda still untouched. We are fast coming to the conclusion that something in the nature of a time limit is required in the War- rmgtoo Town Council. Nine o'clock might be fixed as the absolute maximum of lateness for one sittting. This would give three hours for debate, which is quite as long a time as the members are capable of coherently arguing for at a stretch. The last hour on Tues- day saw things rushed in an undignified and unprofitable fashion, solely owing to the fact that members were tired of hearing one another. If the business cannot all be transacted in a reason- able time, then some such thing as a nine o'clock limit might be enforced and the balance held over for a subseluent special or ordinary meeting. As things are frequently carried on at present, the town would gain rather than lose by such a course of procedure.

There was again a lively debate on the Carnegie library question, and a vety unsatisfactory result. By this time the Council had been sitting some three hours, and the members were getting too tired and impatient to transact the business properly. Councillor Wright, in a strongly-worded speech, proposed the rejection of the minute authorising an appeal to Mr. Carnegie for money required to extend the Library, and in so far as the question of the town's dignity was concerned he had considerable support. Probably, if 'he had enlarged more upon this aspect of the case and said little about what he conceived to be Mr. Carnegie's personal character, he would have achieved his object. The vay in which the millionarie's wealth was amassed is certainly a matter for consideration, but it involves an ethical question apparently (pito above the heads of the members of the Warrington Town Council. Many of them, we fear, do not care how Mr. Carnegie got his money, and if that is the frame of mind in which they approach the subject it is obviously of little use trusting to the niceties of their moral perception. We should have thought, however, that the men who are so proud of the town—and every Town Councillor naturally comes under this head—would have been more concerned about the upholding of its dignity. Bat" the narrowing lust of gold" has warped their views, and when the Mayor closured the debate by asking for " ayes " and

"noes " on the point at issue the" ayes " had it. At any time this is an unsatisfactory way of taking a vote, and it was by no means conclusive on Tuesday. The motion to "open communication with Mr. Andrew Carnegie," however, is pre- sumed to have obtained the approval of the Coun- cil. We hope the Museum and Library Committee will refrain from any indecent haste in writing to the millionaire now they have got the authority of a purely presumptive majority of the Town Coun- cil.

It is a striking coincidence that just as the Warrington Town Council has decided to approach Mr. Carnegie for a grant, the following telegram from an American correspondent appears in the Press :

" New Orleans, Thursday.—The University of Mississippi has refused Mr. Carnegie's offer of 25,000d015. for a library. Mr: Vardammi, the Governor of the State and a member of the board of trustees, said that the university does not want money coined from the blood and tears of the toiling masses." This is yet another indication of the wave of feel- ino which is sweeping over America against the methods by which certain financial magnates have amassed huge fortunes. The decision of the University of Mississippi should provide food for reflection for the Warrington Councillors who are obviously so eager to be handling Mr. Carnegie's money.

Quite an amusing little discussion was gener- ated by gas. The large profit of :U0,407 made by the Gas Department aroused the cupidity of several members. Councillors Lord and Clegg, as representing the shop-keepers, wanted a reduction in the price of gas, which they doubtless find is an expensive commodity to them. Councillor Wilkin- son, as a property owner, firmly opposed the suggestion. He wanted the rates reducing first. Both parties having had their say, the Chairman of the Gas Committee, Alderman Fairclough, remarked humorously that if the Council would reduce the rates he would reduce the price of gas. What he meant was, of course. that a concession from him was dependent on the, rates being re- duced independently of the large contribution made yearly by his department. In making out the Borough Budget a month or two back, the Borough Treasurer credited himself with a pros- pective £9,000 from the Gas Committee, so that there only remains a balance of about £1,400 for the Committee to place to its reserve account. We certainly think a reduction of the rates is more imperative than a lessening of the gas charges. The price of gas will compare very well with the price in most towns, but the rates are so high that they are driving people into the suburbs and pre- judicing Warrington's chances of commercial development.

The Medical Officer of Health, for having again dared to refer to" the widespread nuisance of un- pavod streets and passages" in his annuli report, has incurred the disfavour of Alderman Tinnion, who strongly condemned Dr. Gornall at the Coun- cil meeting on Tuesday, where the ;atter had, of course, no opportunity of replying. In this matter our sympathies are all with the Medical Officer, and we think he is en- titled to the thanks of the public for so fearlessly stating his views while knowing that they would be unpalatable to Alderman Minion and the Paving and Sewerage Committee. As Medical Officer it is Dr. Gornall's duty to inquire into all circumstances which have any bearing on the health of the town, and if the condition of many streets and passages is such as to he dis- tinctly unhealthy then it is equally his duty to point out the fact and the necessity for remedial measures. If he were to go about in fear of giving offence by his report it would be of very little value and the ratepayers won/03 soon conclude they had either a very timorous or inefficient medical officer. We fee/ sure Dr. Gornall will not allow his future course of action to be influenced in any way by the harsh criticism of Alderman Tinnion and his committee. , He is first and foremost the adviser and protector of Warrington's 70,000 inhabitants and not merely the servant of any one committee.

A petty little squabble arose on the minute relating to the illumination of tramcars in honour of the Football Club's victory. A few members raised what they termed a question of princip'e and protested against the money of the ratepayers being spent in this manner. The protest caused a somewhat lively discussion, but that was all the effect it had. There was ample justification for the Mayor and the Electricity Committee in authoris- ing the display. As a business venture it woull probably have paid had the cars covered the route according to schedule time. Moreover, it is a miser- able Town Council that never rejoices, and in rejoicing with the footballers the Council was not by any means contemptuous of the ratepayers. Ninety-nine in every hundred of the latter eppeared to take a pleasurable share in the demonstration quite regardless of the fact that the cost to each one would be so high as one- sixteenth of a penny. We are very glad thac, the Council did do honour where honour was due even at some slight expense to the ratepayers. A Royal visit to the town would involve a very much larger expenditure of rate pap& money, but the small , minority who objected on principle would be laughed out of court—or council. The real and the imaginary situation appear to us to be on all fours with each other.

Warrington' branch Library scheme is at last an accomplished fact, but it is as yet too early to judge whether the public support will realize anticipations. During the summer months there is naturally a decrease in the demand for litera- ture most people preferring to enjoy open-air recreation. By the time that the (larger evenings come round the system will have got into thorough working order, and it should certainly prove a great convenience to the inhabitants of districts which are a considerable distance from the Central Library. About one hundred volumes have been sent to each of the three delivery stations established at the Winwick-street, Church- street and Green-straet Post Offices, and any other books can be obtained at short notice. Although comparatively few books have been issued this week, we hear there has been an encouraging demand for guarantee forms, and if the branch libraries have the eftect of inducing the public to take greater advantage of the supply of wholesome literature, they will have justified their existence

The union between Norway and Sweden was less than a hundred years old. It followed on the " battle of the nations." fought on October 186.3, 1813, when Napolboo, after his retreat on L,ipsic, sustained a sound drubbing, and did not halt froei retreat until he had again crossed the Rhine. The German kingdoms put out their heads again, like snails on a wet evening ; a Sovereign Prince of the Netherlands remounted the throne; and Denmark was compelled to accept the Treaty of Kiel in January of the following year, by which the French alliance was abandoned, that crumbling. little island of Heligoland was ceded to Britain—which some critics say we ought never to have parted with to Germany—and Frederick VI. of Denmark handed over Norway. to Sweden--without asking NorwaY 0 permission—and, for compensation, received the Swedish Pomerania and Rugen. Thus Bernadotte Napoleon's Marshal, in whom had been found e successor to Charles XIII. of Sweden, saw Sweden make a tolerable excha-ge in cutting the cable of her connection with Germany. But at the othBr. end of " the only one of the Napoleonic dynasties which has survived matters are in hardly BO flourishing a state to-day. Sweden has now got neither Pomerania, Rugon, nor Norway However, their are prophets in Isrtel who see better things in store, if Mr. Bjornson, the Norwegian novelist— whose books " Flags er,° Flying in Town and Harbour" and "In Goa a Way" introduced him to English readers—WO be said to be one of them. He looks for an eventual union between Norway, Sweden, and Den- mark. Norway took the field to dispute the undesired union of 1814; it is ardently to be hoped that a settlement of the present difficulty may 128 found without the use of cannon.

.LOCAL NEWS. Coming Events. The anniversary of the Sunday schools connected with the Baptist Church, , Golborne-street, wiil be celebrated on Sunday by the holding of special services. A musical service will be held in the afternoon at which selections will be given by the Legh-street Primitive Methodist choir. The Rev H. Dunnico will preach at the services in the morning and evening. The anniversary services in connection wtth Cairo- street Unitarian Chapel will be celebrated on Sunday. The preacher at the special services which will be held both morning and evening will be the Rev. E. A. Parry, of Liscard. The annual show in connection with the Warrington and District Agricultural Society will be held at Walton Park on the lith,August. Messrs. Fletcher Russell and Co., will next week hold an exhibition of gas cookers in the Parish room, IGrappenhall-road, Latchford. Admission to the ' exhibition, which will open on Tuesday and close on Friday will be free. A feature of the proceedings I will be the lectures on cooking which will be given each day by Mrs. H. M. Young, who is well-known to Warrington audiences. No charge will be made for admission to the lectures.

At the Warrington Police Court yesterday (Friday), Bodelia Garrity, Peers Court, was charged on remand with neglecting her child. —Air. Browne prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C. and stated that the child was in a terrible condition through neglect and was in the workhouse. The Boating Fatalities. At the Parish Church on Sunday evening, a special service was held in memory of Mr. J. J. Dennett, his son John Dennett (8), and Mr. Arthur Tomlinson (23), all of whom resided in Egerton-street, and who lost their lives while boating on the Mersey onSaturday week There was a large congregation. Members of the Order of Foresters with which society the late Mr. Dennett was connected met in the Market-place at about six o'clock and walked in procession to the Parish Church. The Rector preached and took for his text the words " And ye now, therefore, have sorrow but I will see you again, and your heart shall rejoice, and your joy. no man taketh from you" I—St. John xvi. 22. Such cironmstances as those of the terrible accident by which the deceased had lost their lives, he said caused them distress,but good often came out of sorrow, and it might be a warning to many " that in the midst of life we are in death." The life of the deceased man 'Dennett would probably be an example to many, for he had heard from the secre- tary that the brother they had lost was a good man, a good father, and that he was respected by all. They ought so to live that their lives might be an example to all around them. At the conclusion of the service Mr. Darby played the Dead March in" Saul." "PREMIER" CYCLES at 12 guineas with change speed gear itc., unequalled.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge st.,Warrington.—Advt. "The Revival of Gaelic." An interesting and able essay on the above subject has just been pubiished by Mr. H. P. Hughes, of Stockton Heath. It is obviously the result of Much careful research and the author advances valuable practical suggestions for building up a reformed alphabet and orthography, to bring modern Gaelic into line with Welsh-Keltic. Mr. Hughes well points out that in their enthusiasm for the revival of Gaelic, some of its advocates are apt to overlook collateral issues that may have a weighty hearing or influence npon the rising and future generations of Ireland. " For instance, he says, 'much •of the benefit which English capital and the English language have undoubtedly conferred upon Ireland and the Irish people could easily be lost, or, ,at least, depre- ciated in value, unless the new movement is very discreetly handled. From his study on the subject, Mr. Ilughes is convinced that a consider- able proportion of the people of Ireland greatly doubt whether the benefits to be derived, or to be antici- pated, from the teaching of Gaelic will at all, be com- mensurate with the risk of educatiohal and political degeneracy .whieh may accompany it. AS a second language —lust as Welsh is - Mr. Hughes is heartily in sympathy with the Gaelic movement, but, he adds " We Kelts cannot turn back the 'clock of time, nor ought we to permit any man, or section of men, by playing upon our religious, patriotic or political weaknesses, to decoy us into cutting of our nose to spite our face '—that is to say, in plain English to persuade us, by specious arguments, into taking the risk of committing business and commercial (and possibly even national) suicide." Mr. Hughes deals exhaustively with the difficult problems which form themselves round the serious discussion of the move- ment for restoring the Gaelic 'Language, and his essay may be regarded as a valuable contribution on the subject.

UNDER THE CLOCK for Good BOOTS.—Advt. The Tramways. Mr. John Telfer, tramways manager in his annual 'report to the Electricity and Tramways Committee, ' gives at the outset, a number of figures as to the num- ber of passengers carried; and the mileage and receipt showing that the number of passengers carried has increased 369,580, the receipts by £626 ; and the receipts per car mile by nearly id. Mr. Teller says ;—The total working eitpsrtses have decreased £1,142 ; from 675 d. to 6.33 d. per car mile. On inquiry I find that the working costs of 60 muni- cipal tramways is 613 d. per car mile, so I think our costs may be considered satisfactory. The increase of receipts and decrease of working costs has resulted in the gross profit being increased from 44,468 9s. 8d to £6,404 9s. 10d., an increase of £1,938 Os. 2d. This would have paid a percentage of 6.49 per cent. on our capital. After paying interest and sinking fund charges, which have increased £702 there is a Wance of £450 as against a deficit of £7OO last year. Compensation Claims at Warrington.

At the Warrington County Court on—Thurslay before his Honour Judge. Bowen Rowlands, K.C., Robert Conway, collier, Hayaock, brought an action for his Honour's arbritation as to the amount payable by the Collins Green Colliery Company, under an award of compensation for injuries received on the Bth December Mr. Riley(instructed by Messrs. Riley,Kelly and Picton, St. Helens)who appeared for the plaintiff explained that Conway was ruptured on the Bth December, and was paid 6s. a week compensation until the 3rd March. On that date plaintiff retnrned to the colliery to take up light employment and was put to work on the screen, but was incapable of doing the work. Plaintiff asked that his Honour should order the continuance of the pazinent of 6s. per week—a payment which had ceased since March 3rd.—For the respondents Mr. Peace (Messrs. Peace and Ellis, Wigan) submitted ' that the plaintiff was not incapable of follow— ing light employment, and applied for the reduction of the award to the nominal sum of id. per week.—After hearing the evidence his Honour gave judgment for the plaintiff for Os. per week compensation, as ban March last, with costs on scale B.—A second action of a similar character was brought against the Collins Green Colliery Company, by Thomas Greenan,collier,Haydock, who was injured in May of last year. Mr. Riley explained that the applicant was breaking a stone when a piece of the stone hit him on the knee, blood poisoning superven- ing. —Upon the suggestion of his Honour counsel held a consultation on the result of which they. agreed to judgment for the plaintiff for £65 with cost,.

British Women's Temperance Association.

A meeting of this Assoeiation was held in the church parlour at Bold-street on Tuesday, Mrs. Norman pre- siding in the absence of Mrs. Garnett. —An interest- ing report on the ,National Council meetings held in London from May 15th to 19th was submitted by Mrs. Simpson who, along with Mrs. Osborne, represented the Warrington branch. A sympathetic reference was made to the death of Mrs. James Tomkinson, wife of the member for Crewe, who was an active worker in the temperance cause, and was well known as a splendid platform speaker. At the opening meeting in Queen's Hall a resolution 4vas passed calling upon all citizens to work t 'gather for the furthsrance of the following temperance reforms : —(a) The closing of licensed houses on Sundays, and their earlier closing on week-days; (b) such a reversal of the Licensing Act as will restore and extend the power of the licensing authority, and eliminate all vested interest from existing licences at the earliest possible date; (c) the securing of compulsory scientific teach- ing of temperance in all .national schools; (d) the grant to the people of the right of direct pooular veto over the issue or renewal of licencies. At the Council meeting it was reported Ithat the membership had increased by 10,589. Resolutims were passed against the employment of women as barmaids, and the holding of meetings of benefit soc'eties in public houses. Lady Carlisle was unanimously re-elected president; Mrs. Crosfield Was elected vice-president; Mrs. Agnes Stack, lion. cm:responding secretary; Mrs. Osborne, hon. record- ing secretary; Mrs. C. C. Reed, assistant hon. record- ing secretary; Mrs. Bertha Mason, hon. treasurer. Mrs. Simpson concluded her report by stating that as Christian women the members felt it incumbent upon them to do all they could to make good laws in our land. They felt that the time had gone by when it Was necessary to discuss whether a Christian should be a citizen; therefore they took an interest in the political as well as the social life. "PREMIER" CYCLES at 7 guineas startling Value.— J. S. GA.RLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge at., Warrington.- Advt. Beautiful Warrington. In the year 1902 a society was founded in the town of Oldham, called " Beautiful Oldham Society." The society owed its origin to the courage and enthusiasm of an Oldham lady who published in the local press an article entitled " Beautiful Oldham, Why not? " followed shortly by another.. " Beautiful Oldham, shall we try ?" The object of the society, as its name implied, was to promote the beauty of the town in every possible way and to preserve and protect all features of interest. Needless to say, the idea and title of the society met with plenty of ridicule at first. It is gratifying to learn that this has now almost, if not quite disappeared, and that the society now holds an honoured place in the public opinion of the town. Much has been done in the short space of three years. The annual show of bulbs grown in the people houses is now an estab- lished success. The children are being formed intol an association for the protection of birds and flowers. Waste spaces have been made neat and planted. Dis- used burial grounds have been transformed into pleasant gardens. Trees have been planted in streets, and railway embankments have been covered with grass and shrubs. Experiments have been made to find fuel which will send out the minimum amount of black smoke, and much valuable information is being collected on this subject. New spheres of work are constantly presenting themselves, and there seems to be no lack of employment for the members of the Society. But what is more important than actual achievement is the public sentiment which has been created. The idea is taking bold of the people that the town in which they live ought to be 'made as cheerful and beautiful a place as possible. Once let, that idea become firmly rooted in. their minds and it; will bear its fruit in due time. The Oldham scheme is spreading to other Lancashire and Yorkshire towns, while we also hear of • "Beautiful Birmingham" and "Beautiful Leicester;'! and it is to be hoped that Warrington will not be behind-hand in this matter. There are bright spots in the old town which show how much can be one by loving care and genuine interest. We understand on excellent authority that the matter will shortly be brought before the notice of our citizens at a public meeting and we trust that it will be taken up with enthusiasm by all classes, so that the title Beautiful Warrington" may be no misnomer but an abiding reality. •
