STOCKTON HEATH.

PASSIVE RESISTERS GOODS SOLD.

. _ The goods of the Stockton Eleath passive resisters seized in default of payment of the education rate*wore sold by auction byMr.Frank Hickman,in the Warrington market on Monday. In each case thearticie seized was a watch and these were promptly bought in by Mrs. J. S. Hughes for the amounts required to cover the rate and the costs:—Mr. H. Law, 15s. 9d; Rev. J. S. Hughes 14s ; and Rev. H. Minnie° 15s. There was only a very small attendance at the sale which attracted little • notice. After the sale the Rev. J. S. Hughes expressed thanks to the auctioneer and to 'qr. Bradshaw, assistant overseer, for their courtesy and kindness. The resisters he said were willing to pay for education, but they ' objected to have their children instructed in principles which they themselves did not believe in. They taught their children what was their belief, and had to pay for it, and they wanted others to do the same. However, the time was coming when the necessity for protesting in that way would be removed. They could hear the ringing of the death knell of the present Government, and then with a new Government in power, they would have the Education Act considerably amended if not altogether removed. Mr. Law also spoke, and said a scene like the sale which had taken place that day was a disgrace to the country and reflected disgrace on the Government which passed the Act which brought it about. :dr. Hickman returned thanks.

THE LATE MR. JOSEPH: MOUNFIELD.

Mr. Pilling said it was his sad duty to bring before the notice of the committee the death of one of their members. He personally felt very deeply the loss of Mr. Mounfleld with whom he had been associated both there and on the District Council for a long time. He proposed that a vote of condolence be sent to the relatives of Mr. Monnfleld. .

Mr. Clarke said it was his painful duty to second the proposal. Mr. Menufield was an old Warringtonian and was one of the best known men in the district and was a fair dealing and a fair minded man to whom they could all look up. With regard to his usefulness on the committee he was sure Mr. Mounfleld's services had been of great value. His advice had alway been looked to and almost always accepted. Mr. H. J. Westbrook said that if there was one thing in which Mr. Mounfield set an example it was in his regular attendance at those meetings and at the meetings of the District Council and in his conscieatious dis-, charge of his duties.

THE NEW WESLEYAN SCHOOLS. The Clerk read a letter from Messra. Robert Davies and Company, solicitors' writing on behalf of Mr. Frank Warburton, objecting to the new Wesleyan schools being brought up to Heath-street. Mr. Warburton was the owner of the adjoining houses which he had been compelled to set back to the extent of six feet and he considered that if the new schools were brought up to the street it would be detrimental to his property. Having had to set his houses back he felt it was Par and only right that the new buildings should be set back. He (the clerk) had replied that the committee had no building line and in that case permission had been given to bring the buildings up to the street. He was afraid nothing could be done. The architect to the property had informed him that the building was partly up and in that event it was no use them going further in the matter. .

Mr. J. W. Bouchter proposed that in future plans for new buildings should be considered on the evening previous to the meeting of the Parochial Committee. Under the present arrangement of considering plans immediately before the meeting of the Parochial Com- mittee it was impossible to consider the plans properly. Some plans had been passed which the members of the committee afterwards regretted having allowed. (blear, hear.) Mr. Bouchier's motion was carried. THE WATEREiG OF THE ROA.DS.

Mr. H.IJ. Westbrook moved: "That this com- mittee considers the question of watering the District Council roads during dry weather should receive imme- diate attention by the District Council. Mr. Bouchier seconded and the resolution was carried

' ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL. Annual Meeting of the Warrington and District League. Mr. T. P. Bradshaw presided at the annual meeting of this league which was held at the Marquis of Granby Inn, Church-street, on Thursday evening. In presenting the annual report the SECRETARY (Mr. C. T. Cox) stated that the season had been a very good one. The club secretaries had co-operated more with the league officials than ever I,efore, and had saved the League the unpleasant duty of fining and expelling as was the case ast season, through non- compliance with the rules. As would be noticed from the treasurer's report the fines 1234 been much lower than In any previous year. When the season commenced the first division of the league had only nine teams, but he was pleased to say that all remaineil in the competition to the close. The second division started with thirteen clubs and ended with ten. He must congratu- late Warrington Albions and Vulcan for winning their respective leagues iu grand style. The performances of the teams were as follows :—Warrington Albions, played 16, won 14, lost 1, drawn 1, for 41, sgainst 6, points 29 ; ' Vulcan, played 18,won 16, lost 1, drawn 1, for 77, against 20, points 33. He would like the club secretaries to communicate with him RS early as possible re their teams for the coming season, as each one present would remember the trouble they had experienced before through teams joiniug late and causing the fixtures to be re-arranged. An inter-league match with St. Helens and district was played on January 2nd on the War- rington Albions ground and resulted inn draw of two goals each. He expected that next time they met Warrington would win by a good margin. (Applause.) The TREASURER (Mt. W. H. Smalley) reported that the season commenced with a balance in hand of £8 4s. 3d. The total income, including this amount,was £2l Os. 2d. and after paying expenses there was a credit balance of £3 3s. The following officers were re-elected :—President, Mr. W. Chalmers ; vice-presidents,Messrs. F. J. Whittle and H. Isherwood; chairman, Mr. T. P. Bradshaw; hon secretary, Mr. C. T. Cox ; hon. treasurer, Mr. W. H. Smalley ; auditors, Messrs. J. J. Kite and W. Marley. It was decided that a grant of £1 10s. be made to the hon. secretary for his past services. An adjourned 'Meeting was fixed for July 27th. I After the meeting an enjoyable smoking concert was held. Several members of the company rendered songs, and gramaphone selections by Mr. Allen and solos on the" bones " by Mr. Parry were well received. During an interval the presentation of medals took place. Mr. T. P. Bradshaw' who presided, thanked members for electing him to the office of chairman for ithe tenth year in succession. It was , his intention at the close of the forthcoming season to retire from tak- ing an active part in football. He did not think there was a league conducted on better lines than the Warrington League. He was pleased to say that during the season there had been from 50 to 75 per cent, less cases of misconduct to deal with, which showed that the game had been played in a better spirit. Mr. Bradshaw referred to several alterations in the game which were suggested. He also replied to the criticism of the League's action in having the " Observer " Cup final played on a Rugby ground. The success of that com- petition, he said, depended upon the support they obtained from Rugby enthusiasts, and he considered the action of the League quite justifiable. New applications for admission to the League had been received from Helsby, L. and N.-W. St. Helens Rees, Oughtrington Park, and Kastner-Kelner. (Applause). Mr. W. CHALMERS, president of the League, presented the gold medals gained by Warrington Albions to Mr. E. Bullen, the captain, and gold-centred medals to Mr. Day, captain of the Vulcan team.

IMPORTANT FRIENDLY SOCIETY APPEAL.

Bro. David Houghton and the Order of

Druids.

A;case of interest and importance to members of Friendly Societies was concluded by the Board of Mana,gement of the Druids Society in Manchester on Saturday. The Warrington branch of the Society had expelled Bro. David Houghton' the Vice Grand Master of the Order, and the late D.C.S. of the District, on certain charges which had been brought against him. Against the expulsion Bro. Houghton appealed to the Board, and the appeal was commenced a week ago, and concluded on Saturday.—Mr. T. S. Steel, solicitor, Warrington' appeared for the appellant; and the War- rington District of Druids was represented by Bro. Edwin Allen, D.C.S. ; Bro. Wm, Mulkey, ; and the Trustees Bros. Wm. Bibby, George Roughley and Samuel Hankey. At the conclusion of the case, in which Bro. Houghton ' stoutly denied the charges, the Board considered its decision. When the parties concerned were re-admitted ,to the room, it was announced that the Grand Master ' had submitted to the Board certain questions to which , they had given their answers. The questions and answers were as follows 1. Has Bro. Houghton withheld receipts ?—lt is not proven. 2. Has he charged Lodges for members long out of benefit ?—lf there were any such charges they were in consequence of Lodge Secretaries returns being faulty. 3. Has he overdrawn the amount due to him for salary ?—No. 4. Has he paid to the Board of Management for more members than were actually on the books of the District?—There is no satisfactory evidence of any such payments. 5. Did he pay the Friendly Societies Council ss. too much ?—Yes ; but it was authorised by the resolu- tion of the District meeting. 6. Were the registers badly kept?—No. 7. Has he detained any Lodge quarterly returns ?-No. 8. Has he debited Management Fund with amounts that belong to the Sick Fund ?—No. It was announced that the appeal was unanimously allowed, and Bro. D. Houghton was re-instated to full membership and his oflice of Vice Grand Master.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Warrington indulged in another friendly-match on Saturday, Werneth being the visitors to the Arpley enclosure. The match was an interesting one but neither eleven had the satisfaction of scoring a victory the result being a draw slightly in favour of Warring- ton. Taking first turn at the wicket the home eleven were all dismissed for 146, and Werneth had res- ponded with 78 for- the loss of 6 wickets when the time limit was reached.

The feature of the Warrington innings was the brilliant display by J. Thompson who made 41 in faultlesi style. J. W. Bibby was also in capital form and compiled 20, while Mason, the professional, knocked up 15.

On the Werneth side F. Hurst was the most success- ful batsman, his innings realizing 27. Mason bowled most effectively for Warrington, capturing 4 wickets for 29 runs.

Warrington second are to be congratulated upon an exceptionally easy victory over Werneth second. E. G. Woods and E. Pierpoint shared the batting honours, knocking up 67 and 53 respectively. Pier- point hit seven boundary strokes while Woods' per- formance included eleven fours. Lowe and Bennett rendered yeoman service with the ball, for while Lowe accounted for five wickets for 33 inns, Bennett captured four wickets for 63.

• Sankey Wire Mills are having a very successful season, and on Saturday they triumphed over Hindley Green. F. Taylor (20) headed'the batting list, while Gleave and Brough bowled exceptionally well, captur- ing four wickets for 18 runs and three wickets for 22 respectively.

Cheshire Lin‘as Engineers severely trounced Man- chester Central, A \Val-burst (51), J. Duckworth (25) and F. Massey (19) batting in grand style. Deeley and Monks were responsible for the early Collapse of the losers, their respective analyses being E. Deeley, 9 overs 3 maidens 14 runs 7 wickets; J. Monks, 8 o—ers 3 maidens 6 runs 2 wickets.

Bowling.

To-day will witness the commencement of the second round of the Works Competition for the Finlayson Cup. The contest so far has produced some excellent play and in this respect matters should even improve as the final is approached. The draw for the second round has resulted as follows : —Crosfield's or Rylands (No. 1) v. Pearson and Knowles' (No. 2); Stubbs v. Lonpon and Aforth-Western ; Richmonds's v. Lmgland: Thewlis and Griffiths' v. Whitecross (No. 1); Rylands' Netting v. Corporation; Rylands' (No. 2) v. Fletcher's; Pearson and Knowles.' (No. 1) v. C.L.C. The matches to be played to-day are Crosfield's v. fly-land's (No. 1) first round, and Stubbs' v. London and North-Western second round.

The two matches which were deeided last Saturday I were productive of' more than usually close games though in each the expected result was arrived at, and Fletcher's and Pearson and Knowles (No. 2) qualified for the second round. Fletthers did not attain their object without a particularly stiff struggle with Forshaw as proved by a final margin of 6 points, Forshaw set off with a lead of 9 point, which T. No matches are down for decision to-day the after- noon having been set apart for the holding of, the _ second of the series of mains. Play will be over the Bull's Head green.

The third annual handicap in connection with the Prince of Wales Lodge of Odafellows, for the Cros- field cup and other prizes will be held on the 22nd July. The green will be selected later.

Nothing has as yet been done with regard to the gift of £5 by Ar. E. England for a competition f,r the championship of Warrington. it will be remembered that the Warrington League, in whose hands the question is placed, advised the holding of a com- petition open to two players connected with each of the League clubs. This idea did not commend itself to Mr. England who recomMended the throwing open of the competition to all clubs in the town. This would necessarily entail a great amount of work to which League officials do not feel equal. What the ultimate arrangement will be I cannot say, but it ought to be possible to arrange a handicap on the lines suggested by Mr. England.

At last the date for the opening of the Corporation Bowling Oren in Victoria Park has been fixed, and June 22nd, if the day be fine, will witness the first occasion upon which the green will be played upon by the general public. The charges also have been fixed and are, for use of bowls and use of green 2d. for the first hour and Id. for each succeeding hour. For players using their own woods the charge will be 2d_ ' for two hours and Id. per hour afterwards. This means that in any event there Will be a minitnuna charge of 2d, for the use of the green. The price ia reasonable enough though it would have been better had the Corporation fixed the minimum at id. instead of at 2d., as the latter figure will prove somewhat of a detriment. The venture is however a sort of busi- ness speculation and if it proves to be a profitable one, no doubt the Corporation will see their way to redace the charge.

Water Polo.

Warrington played their opening league match of the season pa Tuesday evening, and suffered the 'dis- appointment of a defeat at the hands of St. Helens. The home team. made no mistake about it, either, for they piled on ten goals against the Warringtonians solitary one. In the first half Warrington, although defending the shallow end, could ' not notch a single point, while their opponents p!aced ten goals to their credit. On resuming Walker scored for Warrington who were thus spared the ignominy of being "

On Tuesday evening next Warrington will again meet St. Helens at the Corporation Baths, Leigh-street, while the second team will try conclusions with Chester The occasion will be a particularly interesting one, as Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall will attend for the pur- pose of presenting the challenge shield to the winners of last season's works competition. There will be an. exhibition squadron race between the champions and a picked team, and a two length's contest for club mem- bers will also be decided.

Cycling

Ottlton Park was the destination of the Warrington 'Cycling Club on Saturday. The weather NIMA beautifully fine and the roads were in excellent order after the light showers of the previous day. Fifteen members turned out for the grand run through Delamere forest and the outing proved highly enjoy- able. Tea was served at the Red Lion Inn and on the return journey several of the wheelers looked in at the fete at Dutton Hall.

On Tuesday evening the weather was dull and threatening, with the resaltt that only a dozen members made the journey to picturesque old Warburton.

A WORD TO THE WISE.

The fickle nature of the climate of this country often gives rise to a variety of ailments and complaints, which assuming at first the form of only a slight indisposition, if neglected and unheeded may become the germ of serious diseases, which will undermine the constitution, and finally result in a long and lingering illness, and perhaps terminate fatally. The variable temperature and the changeable weather of the last month or so, makes it incumbent on all of us to exercise wise and prudent precautions to counteract the evil effects which the weather may have had upon our health and comfort. Already we frequently hear such complaints as "No appetite," "These frequent headaches," "This languid feeling," and dozens of other expressions which all point to the urgent need of a good Tonic. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

THEFT OF FLOWERS FROM BANK PARK. From time to time the Warrington authorities have had cause to complain of persons helping themselves to flowers growing in the public parks, and a special wateh has been kept with a view to putting a stop to the practice. On Monday two lads, William Mitton, 8 David-street, and James Kennedy, 12, Pierpoint-street, were charged at the Warrington Polic3 Court with picking rhododendron blossoms from Bank Park. Both pleaded guilty.—The Chief Constable stated that on the evening of May 28th, Detective Clough was on duty in Bank Park when he saw the two defendants with some newly plucked rhododendron blossoms. The boys on seeing Clough went and hid themselves. Clough however, caught one and questioned him, the lad ad- mitting that he had taken the flowers from the park. A redeeming feature of the case was that the lads bad not attempted to tell any lies.—The lads expressed their sorrow for what they had done and the cases against them were dismissed upna payment of costs.

Warrington Borough Pollee Court.

Minor Cases

-- MONDAY.—Before the Mayor (in the chair), Alderman Smethurst, Councillor Wilkinson, and Messrs. W. Roberts, H. Thornton, G. Piling, S. Wright, and

J. W. Hayward. A Disc RICEFUL SCENE.—Sarah Ann Dodd, 11, Stephen-street, was summoned for drunkenness.—P.C. Tolley said that on Tuesday he was on duty in the Cemetery when the three persons who lost their lives in the boating accident were being buried. The defendant was drunk and was looking over the wall in Stephen- street and shouting, and otherwise distracting the attention of the mourners.—Defendant, who did not appear, was fined ss. and costs. CRUELTY TO A HORS.—George Leather and Michael Caulfield, both ot Leigh, were summoned for illtreating a horse, Leather by working it in an unfit condition, and Caulfield by causing it to be worked.—lnspector Gibson (R.S.P.C.A.) stated that he saw Leather driving the horse. The animal was suffering from inflammation in one of its legs and was in great pain.—Caulfield agreed to have the horse destroyed.—He was fined ss. while Leather was fined 2s. 6,-1.

WORKINGMEN'S DWELLINGS. A Flourishing Warrington Building Society. A suceessful year's work was under review at the thirty-fifth annual general meeting of members of the Warrington Workingmen's Permanent Benefit Building Society which was held in Golborne-steeet schoolroom on Monday evening. Mr. J. W. Bouchier presided and I the attendance included :—Messrs. James Harding, 1 chairman, E. Wallington, R. Isherwood, J. Baxter, W. H. Wallington, W. J. Bate, H. Baines, T. Bowers, J. Bell W. Charnock and G. Cox(manages),Mr. Josiah Longland (solicitor), and Mr. Peter Dalton (secretary). THE REPORT. In their annual report the Managers stated that during the year the sum of £1,597 7s. 4d. had been received as subscriptions, £2,270 Is. Ild, for repayments and £1 10s. sd. entrance fees,fines, &c., making the total receipts £3.871 19s. Bd. ; the working expenses being £69 Os. lid. The withdrawals amounted to £1,338 16s. 11d. The sum of £2,900 had been advanced to mem- bers. There were 216 subscribers, holding 2891 shares; and 40 borrowers' holding 203:1 shares; making 256 members, holding 4931 shares. The bonuses this year amounted to £507 10s. on the amount lent, of which sum £92 16s. was divided as profit, and £414 14s. carried to the prospective interest account. The total amount remaining owing upon mortgages was £10,957 55., of which only £9,632 les. 4d. could be regarded as capital, the balance of .£1324 Bs. Bd. representing the propor- tionate amount of interest thereon, calculated for the number of years the several mortgages had still to run. The profits for the year, including £l9 ss. 7d. from last account, after deducting working expenses, amounted to £329 19s. 4d. out of which the Managers recom- mended a dividend of 4 per cent, which would take £352 9s. 5d., leaving a balance of £4O Os. 11d., £25 of which they directed to be placed to credit of the Con- tingent Fund,and the balance, £l5 9s. Ild., carried for- ward to next year's account. The whole of the managers retired. Messrs. Harding, E. Wallington, Isherwood, Bouchier, W. EL Wallington and Baxter, were eligible and offered themselves for re-election. Six additional Managers would be taken from the register in rotation. A SOUND POSITION. The CHAIRMAN remarked that considering the state ,of trade the weekly payments bad been fairly large, and the result of the year's work might be considered satis- factory. The society had made sufficient to pay a dividend of 4 per cent and he believed that under the new rules that was about the utmost the society could pay. They might certainly regard it as a safe invest- ment at 4 per cent. They hair ample reserve to cover not only all the deposits, but to pay interest on those deposits so long as the society might have to run supposing that it went into liquidation. The cash hand at the bank of over 60 remained after the dividend had been paidif the society had been able to act as they had for something like thirty years—divide the profit made during the year after making sufficient allowance for contingency and reserve funds—they might have paid 5.1 per cent. The working expenses amounted to .£6O Os. 11d., which was equal to only 135.10 d. on every £lOO that the society had dealt with. On the motion of Mr. GREALEY, seconded by Mr. Massey, the report was adopted.

_ , THE DIVIDEND. It was decided, on the proposition of Mr. GREALEY, seconded by Mr. CHARNOCK, to pay a dividend of 4 per cent.

ELECTIONS. Messrs. J. Harding, E. Wallington. R. Isherwood, 3. W. Bouchier, J. Baxter, and W. H. Wallington were re-elected managers of the Society. Messrs. Wm. Bodle, jun., and Robert Lee were re-elected surveyors, and Messrs. A. W. Howard and G. Armstrong, auditors.

WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL

ALDERMAN BENNETT AND THE SMOKE NUISANCE.

Medical Officers' Report Criticised.

INGREAsE OF SALARIES GRANTED.

So much business had to bd transacted at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Town Council, which was held on Tuesday that the meeting lasted four hours, and even then the whole of the business was not disposed of. The Mayor presided and there were also present the Deputy Mayor (Alderman Fairclough), Aldermen A. Bennett, John Evans, R. W. Francomb, 'T. Hutchin- son J. Smethurst, D. Tinnion, Councillors T. H. Ashcroft, T. Barton, W. Benson T. Burton, W. Clegg, D. S. Duncan, James Evans, L Eilison,'W. J. Forshasv, T. J. Hulse. Dr. Joseph, J. Lord, J. Maginn, A. Peacock P. Peacock, J. Pemberton, J. Roberts, J. Sankey, J. Skelland, J. F. Smith, F. Stott, J. Williams, W. J. Wilson, T. Wright; with Messrs. J. L. Whittle (Town Clerk), J. Fairhurst (Borough Treasurer), T. Longdin, (Boren gh Surveyor)J. Deas (Water Engineer),C. Madeley ( Librarian), W.S. Haddock (Gas Engineer),J . G. Gornall (Medical Officer).

===== 0002601_19050610_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
REPRESENTATIVE MANAGER APPOINTED.

On the motion of Alderman BENNETT, seconded by Councillor SANKEY, Mr. John Taylor was appointed a representative manager of the Holy Trinity National Schools in place of the late Alderman Sutton. ALDERMAN BENNETT AND THE SMOKE NUISANCE. "A DISGRACE TO CIVILIZATION." I ALDERMAN TINNION'S INDIGNATION.

When the minutes of the Health Committee were presented for confirmation Alderman Bennett made his hardy anneal speech with regard to the smoke nuisance. In his annual report the Medical Officer said " The smoke nuisance is, as might be expected, very bad, and the people so devoted to money-getting as not to be concerned much by the evils to health arising therefrom still less caring for the ugliness of it." That paragraph he thought was a disgrace to civilization and reflected very strongly upon the public, spirit in the town. He really thought it a tenible state of things that at the beginning of the 20th century it should be possible for the Medical Officer to write that paragraph. He noticed the omission for the report of any statistics with regard to the steps taken by the responsible committee with a view to the abating of the nuisance. Whether there were statistics or not anyone who is familiar with the town would realise that no steps were taken to abate the nuisance, whioh was a detriment to the beauty and health of the town, and the happiness of the whole community. He spoke with great assurance, because he was fortified by the experience of the Electricity and Tramways Committee, who were formerly one of the greatest offenders in the town but who had now got rid of the nuisance altogether, and by doing so had decreased their cost for fuel. Surely if gentlemen in the town were amenable to no other argu- ment when they saw the possibility of Obtaining bigger dividends they would consider the question and do something to abate the nuisance. If anything conid be done every self-respecting committee of the town should see that it should be done to put the law into operation until that chronic disgrace had been abated. Alderman TINNION said he must protest against the mairer in which Dr. Gornall had referred to the paving of streets. Great efforts had been made during the year to cope with the difficulty though it was not owing in any degree to what Dr. Gornall or anyone else had said. The doctor suggested that they really wanted some more definite system under which it would not be possible for streets and passages to remain so long in an unsatisfactory state. He supposed it was the doctor's modesty which prevented his suggesting he should have superintended the paving of the streets. If Dr. Gornall did so however, he did not think they would get any more satisfactory results. Since last September they have paved 22 streets and passagep over three-quarters of a mile in length at a cost of £1,848, 41 one mile and a fifth in length at a cost of £5,251, and had prepared to bring before the Local Government Board, 78 streets 2i miles in length at a cost of £9,406. He thought that after the assur- ance which was given that the committee would do al It could to overtake the unpaved streets, the doctor might not have gone out of his way as he bad done to refer to the streets. If the doctor was treated as he treated his brother officials his position would be very difficult, and for him to make objections in his annual report which went to the Local Government Board, was unfair to his brother officials who had no opportunity of being heard. They could give an explanation to the ratepayers of the borough and to the Council but the borough isurveyor had no opportunity to give an explanation to the Local Government Board. He thought Dr. Gornall would have been better advised and would have acted, more fairly if he had consulted his 'colleagues before giving his report. On behalf of the Paving and Sewerage Committee and of the borough surveyor he protested against the statement made by the medical officer. ' Councillor STorr hoped that Dr. Gornall would not be influenced by anything Alderman Tinnion or the Paving and Sewerage might say but that he would speak the truth as he believed it to he with regard to certain matters. He quite agreed with the medical officer that some better method could be adopted whereby streets should not be allowed to remain unpaved for so long a time. He did not think it was possible for the committee to have done more than it had done during the past 12 months but he thought a better method could be adopted which would assist the committee in overtaking the unpaved streets. He hoped Dr. Gornall would not be influenced by any criticism but would continue to speak fearlessly and without hesitation. Councillor PEMBERTON thought that in his report Dr. Gornall might speak truthfully and at the same time might take the opportunity of ascertaining from Mr. Longdin what was practicable. The Medical Officer might be a good doctor, but he did not think he knew much about the paving of streets. There was a great deal more work in his department which might be looked after to the great benefit of the ratepayers rather than he should waste his time in talking about the streets that were being paved quicker than was ever done before. If Councillor Stott wished the paving of the streets to be conducted on some other method he must get the law altered. The streets in Warrington were being paved as rapidly and as well as in any town he knew. (Hear, hear.) Certainly, some streets might be made more presentable than they were at the present moment, but that was not the fault of the , Paving and Sewerage Committee. The committee felt strongly that the doctor had gone out of his way to make an unjustifiable attack on the committee. He (Councillor Pemberton) could not think the doctor had gone round and examined the streets. Dr. Gornall and his staff would have been better employed in looking after other matters ; for instance, where there were nuisances being committed in paved streets, such as rubbish and refuse accumulating, and decayed vegetable matter, which were serious causes of disease. Councillor Evaxs, alluding to Alderman Bennett's statement with reference to smoke, said it should be remembered that Warrington manufactures consisted principally of iron, and that there was no such thing as applying mechanical stoking to a puddling furnace. (Alderman Bennett " Mood gas.") That was a new thing. He had thought the alderman was a man of patience=(laughter)--and he should give the manu- facturers an opportunity of experimenting before he hastily concluded that they were not doing their best. He believed the manufacturers were doing their best not to create the smoke nuisance Alderman Bennett complained about. The minutes were then agreed to. THE PAVING OF STREETS.

In the minutes of the Paving and Sewerage Committee was a long list of streets to be paved. The minutes also contained a letter from the Water Engineer yequesting that the quantity of water taken from the Appleton mains for street-watering purposes be diminished, and it was resolved that in the opinion of the committee it was most important that the streets should be regularly watered, and that with a view to an alteration in the present conditions of supply, the Chairman, Councillor Burton, and Councillor Evans be appointed a sub-committoe to wait upon the Water Committee at their next meeting. Alderman TINNiom said it as altogether unfair to

make such a request to the Council considering that they were large consumers of water, and paid 9d. per ton for it. The Water Committee should substitute Winwick water where there was a shortage of Appleton water, but the Engineer did not say that they could have a quantity from the Winwick water mains. It was a serious matter, because it prevented the proper water- ing of the streets, and would increase the cost of cartage besides. The minutes were confirmed. BRANCH LIBRARIES OPENED.

In moving the adoption of the minutes of the Museum Committee, Alderman BENNETT remarked that several branch libraries had been opened in Church-street, Winwick-street and Green-street and a small supply of suitable books sent to them. He hoped that inhabitttnts of lthose districts would take advantage of them and justify the expenditure of the committee upon them. Councillor ELLisos seconded, and the minutes were adopted.

OPEN-AIR SWIMMING BATHS. Councillor STol`e asked the Chairman of the Town Hall, Park and Baths Committee: 1. Has the Com- mittee yet considered the question of providing open- air swimming baths, in accordance with the resolution unanimously passed by the Council at a meeting held on October 25th, 1904? 2. If the question has been considered, what decision has the committee arrived at? If the question has not yet been considered, will he undertake that the question shall receive the considera- tion of the committee at an early date, and report their decision to the Council. The MAYOR said that he did not care for the manner in which the questions had been put, but he would answer Councillor Stott as a matter of courtesy. Coun- cillor Stott could have had a reply to them if he had cared to ask the speaker on the quiet. There was no need to have put all that expense of printing on the ratepayers. The answer to the first question was "Yes." In reply to questions two and three, he could say that no suitable place had yet been found to put up an open- air bath. SCHOOL CHILDREN AND THE BATHS. At a meeting of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Com- mittee, held on the 25th May, a deputation from the School Management Sub-committee, consisting of the Rev. Morley Stevenson, Mr. Alderman Bennett, Mr. John Upson, and the Director of Education, attended the committee with reference to the terms upon which school children would be allowed the use of the first class plunge bath, and after discussion it was resolved that the committee allow school children to use the first-class plunge bath on Tuesdays, Wednesdays and Fridays, from 3.30 to 6 p.m., on payment by the Educa- tion Committee of £5O per annum, subject to a teacher being in charge of the children. The deputation inti- mated their willingness to accept these terms. The MAYOR proposed and Alderman SMETHURST seconded the adoption of the minutes. Alderman BENNETT thought that while it was extremely desirable that they should have conditions of cleanli- ness he thought every child should be taught to swim, and personally would not rest satisfied until they gave girls the same facilities as boys. The minutes were approved. THE ORFORD-LANE RECREATION aROUND. At a meeting of the Markets Committee a letter was read from the open spaces sub-committee requesting this committee to discontinue the letting of the Orford- lane recreation ground for any purposes It was resolved that the committee comply with such request. Councillor STOTT hoped the resolution would be strictly adhered to and the minutes were approved. GAS PROFITS. A REDUCTION IN PRICE ASKED FOR. The Borough Treasurer's balance sheet respecting the Gas Department for the year ended 31st March, 1905, shows a net profit of £10,407 lie. Bd. It was resolved that the Chairman, Alderman Evans, and Councillors Evans and Roberts be appointed a sub-committee to' open'and report on the tenders for coal and cannel and other requirements. The committee resolved that the following amended scale of extra discount allowed on gas for motive power to consumers using gas engines over two hours daily and not less than 70 days per quarier, be approved :—Three hours per day id. per thousand „• four hours' 2d.; five hours, 3d.; six hours, 4d. • ten hours, 5d.; 11 hours, 6d. The adoption of the minutes -of the Gas Committee was proposed by Alderman FAIRCLOUGH, and seconded by Councillor ROBERTS. Councillor LORD asked if it was ,contemplated to lower the price of gas to consumers as the profits were so large. The price of electricity had been reduced, and he thought that the gas consumers were entitled to a reduction. Councillor CLEGG said that they were manufacturing gas at something just over Is. per thousand feet but, were charging 2s. 6d. per thousand feet fo,r it. He thought that a reduction should be made in its price instead of the huge profit made from the gas plant , being devoted to the relief of the rates. He hoped that the Committee would bring the matter forward at their next meeting. - The MAYOR stated that the profits on the gas under- taking belonged to the ratepayers while a large number ' of consumers were not ratepayers Councillor PEMBERTON said that Widnes sold gas at is. sd. and were giving more gas for their money to the slot-meter consumers. The conversion into slot- meters in Warrington were going on very slowly. Some means should be devised by which the Gas Committee couid make a reduction. The Mayor might smile, but the people who paid for the gas felt very sore. It particularly affected small shopkeepers who paid rates as well. _ _ _

resulted in such a reduced pressure that it was difficult to get the water to the higher parts of the town. He was quite willing, and probably the committee would be quite willing, to find all the water required—not from one hydrant—but spread over a number. If they were short of Appleton water there appeared to be no reason, to him why they should not be supplied from Winwick. (Hear, hear). PHYSICAL INSTRUCTION OF SCHOOL CHILDREN.

Alderman MINION in moving the minutes of the Gymnasium Committee referred at length to the delib- erations of the committee and of the Education Com- mittee with regard to the physical instruction of school children explaining that the Education Committee had decided to have sach instruction given at the schools by teachers. He theight that it would be infinitely better for the childr to be instructed in the valuable subject of physical drill properly, aud he contended that it was not within the capacity of the teachers in the schools to give such instruction. The Gymnasium Committee felt that the instruction should be given under the direction of their instructors, but under the Education Committee's scheme that had been entirely eliminated. Instruction given by teachers would not be so valuable as that given by the committee's intruc- tors as they had had more experience. Alderman BENNETT said he was sorry if the Gymnasium Committee had thought that the School Management Su's-committee had failed to appreciate the services the ,Gymnasium Committee had rendered to them in the past. He pointed out that out of 280 teachers 169 were fully (minded to give instruction in physical drill, and it was thought desirable that the children should be drilled in their own schools and by their own staffs. There would be a saving in time and possibly in other ways by adopting this method. The MAYOR thought that a gymnasium instructor was more qualified to teach physical drill than any teacher. The walk from the schools to the Gymnasium would in itself be beneficial to the children. Alderman Tfirgrost, in reply, pointed out that the new scheme had been intended for the schools in the neigh- bourhood of the Gymnasium more particularly. The playgrounds of the schools were not adapted for physical drill in the winter when there was two inches of snow on the ground, and other facilities were lacking. The rsinutea of the committee were adopted: PROFITS OF THE ELECTRICITY AND TRAM- WAYS UNDERTAKINGS. At a meeting of the Electricity and Tramways Com- mittee, the Borough Treasurer's balance sheet for the year ending 31st March, 1905, was presented and showed that the net profit on the Electricity Department was £1,513 12s. 9d., and that the year's working on the Tram- ways Department showed a profit of .2450 Bs. 6d., which reduced last year's deficit of £692 75.7 d. to £241 19s. Id. The Elect;:icity and Tramways Committee resolved that the consumers of electrical energy be allowed the option of a flat rate at 41d. per unit with a discount of id. per unit if the account is paid within one month after demand. It was further resolved that the tram lines in Man- chester-road be extended to the far side of Padgate- lane, and that the Borough Surveyor be requested to undertake,the work. Alderman SMETHURST, in moving the confirmation of the minutes said there had been many complaints to the effect that the trams could Rot be seen by people coming from Padgate-lane, and it had been resolved that the lines be exteaded to the far side of Padgate-lane. It would only cost £lOO or £l5O. The profits on the tramways had been described as a "fairy tale," but he thought it was a fairy tale with which they could all fairly well be pleased. A. re-arrangement of the staff had brought abOut a reduction in wages of £991. The price of current water &c., had brought about a reduc- tion of £734. while the receipts from fares had increased by £626. In the electricity department a cost of £3OO had been transferred to the Sanitary Works Committee as the Electricity Committee found that they were doing work which they had no right to do. There had been an increase in the income of £1,780. INCREASES OF SALARY FOR ELECTRICITY AND TRAMWAY OFFICIALS. The committee recommended that the salary of Mr. Telfer, tramways manager, be increased from £l3O to £l5O per annum-, that the salary of Mr. F. W. Purse, assistant electrical engineer, be increased from £156 to £l7O per annum; and that the salary of Mr. W. E. Rogers, mains engineer, be increased from £l3O to £l4O per RRIAHR. Councillor P. Pitscocrt moved an amendment that the proposed increase of Mr. Telfer's salary be referred back. He pointed out that as regarded the tramways department there would have been a deficit if the committee had not cut down expenses by discharging a large number of men. In the electricity depart- ment the use of mechanical stokers and the arrangement with the Sanitary Works Committee accounted for a large saving. He did not therefore think the officials could claim credit for the profit which had been made. A resolution to increase the salaries was brought forward at the February meeting and defeated and he did not think the Council should be pestered with the matter until twelvemonths had elapsed. He did not think the committee had any money to spare, but if they had they might give the train drivers and conductors the extra quarter time on Sunday whieh they had asked for. Councillor`EyaNs seconded. Councillor ASHCROFT said Mr. Telfer had an increase of £lO quite recently, and he did not see why another should he asked for. Councillor Loan said Mr. Telfer was engaged at a salary of £l2O to increase by annual increments of .210 to a maximum of £l5O. It was now proposed to give him the maximum,. and he (Councillor Lord) did not . . . .

Councillor WrrAussoN : These advances of salary are only what they would be entitled to in a private firm.

The voting was as follows For the amendment :—Councillors Evans, Barton, Benson, Lord, Pemberton, Wright, A. Peacock and P. Peacock-8. _

Against :—The Mayor, Aldermen Fairclough, Smethurst, Evans, Hutchinson, Francomb, Tinnion, Councillors Roberts, Wilkinson, Magian, Dr. Joseph, Fulton-Smith, Sankey, Ashcroft, Clegu, Ellison-16. The increases were therefore allowed. THE QUESTION OF DEPRECIATION.

Alderman EVANS said that regarding the saving the Electricity and Tramways Committee had effected as the result of their new arrangement with the Sanitary Committee, as shown in their annual report, he might say that the Sanitary Committee had had to put down in their estimate £6OO as extra cost to them under the arrangement.. As there appeared to be considerable doubt as to the amount of depreciation end reserve setaside by electric supply undertakings, the Engineer had prepared a statement showing the percentage of depreciation and reserve of some 15 metropolitan companies and 42 pro- vincial electric companies. The aggregate capital of those companies was £16,195,401 and the average amount set aside for depreciation and reserve was at the rate of 14 per cent. The MAYOR said the Town Clerk had informed him that the Corporation had not power to set aside money for depreciation but only to keep a reserve fund. Alderman SMETHURST pointed out that during the year over £BOO had been spent in repairs on the tramways and over £BOO at the electrichl works. The minutes were confirmed.

This stroke, how- ever, requires playing carefully, as the striker is somewhat handicapped by having to play over the red ball. When the cannon is made, the red, will generally be left in good position for a pot ar. an in-off, and often .either stroke maybe on.

The stroke shown at B on Diagram 112 would be just an ordinary screw-back cannon were it not for the fact that the object white is kept at tho top of the table by means of a rebound from the lower angle of the centre pocket. Such a stroke if; not nearly as uncommon as many players might imagine, and is a well-known stroke with professionals and good amateurs. The shot is not by any means difficult, as the strength of the stroke prevents the object white front entering the pocket even when it strikes the angle inside the fall of the slate. In fact, if the pocket be a standard one, it is almost impossible to pot the ball at this angle with a strong stroke. In order, however, to allow of any latitude in the manner of catching the white, the Ned must be gloss at hand.

BY THE CLUBMAN.

Cycle Camping.

It is now six years since Mr. T. H. Holding Published a brightly written and remarkable little book, entitled Cycle and Camp,' in which he described his practical experiences of portable Cycle camping. To-day, as a result of Mr. Holding's effort, and his subsequent exertions, we have the Cycle Campers' Association, a body boasting 200 Members, which holds several organized Portable cycle camps every year.

What is cycle camping? In two words, scientc savagery. By its aid it is possible, as has been demonstrated by Mr. Holding and several of his followers, for a couple of cyclists to tour in almost any part of the British Islands, independent of the existence or otherwise of hotels, and to fare well at an expenditure of about 15s. a week per person. The . entire equipment for two persons, including -tent, • Cooking appliances, Sc.c., weighs 14 lb., which, of course, is divided between the two bicycles. The initial cost of a cycle-camping outfit for two persons is about lbs., yet, strange to say, there . is not at present any firm from which it can be Procured complete. The various articles have to be made, or purchased from various sources. Needless to say, cycle camping cannot be carried out without a considerable amounti of work inseparable from camping existence; but the Pastime confers on its votaries an independence which is not without its charm. The results that are obtainable from the phenomenally light and . compact equipment Mr. Holding has evolved, are • calculated to surprise those who have never studied the subject. For example, the writer was recently at a dinner whe4 Mr. Holding was invited to exhibit his latest chtf-rtreuvre in tent construction. The tent, made of Japanese silk, weighed 11 oz., and, together with its lines and pegs, when rolled up, was easily concealed in the pocket of a dress coat. The necessary poles, telescoped into a stout- looking walking-stick, weighing under a pound In the course of a few minutes the structure was pitched, and two full-sized men introduced them- selves beneath its shelter. Of course, a waterproof ground sheet is used, and pillows, &c., provided. Ordinarily, these tents are made of lawn, and- weight about 2 lb. Aword, in conclusion, about the pioneer of cycle camping may shed a light on bow he came to elaborate his remarkable scheme. The President . of the Cycle Campers' Association, now well over fifty years of age, commencf,kl camping on the Prairies of America when nine years old. In the sixties he became an enthusiastic canoeist, and commenced his practical experience of portable camping. Catching the cycle fever later, Mr. Holding, in 1875, originated the proposal which led to the 'foundation of the Cyclists' rouring Club. When the advent of the safety and the pneumatic tyre rendered the plan feasible, Mr. Holding applied his store of knowledge to cycle camping, with the : Practical and gratifying results already cited.

Long-distance Riding.

A long-distance ride is a vague description of an . effort which may be expected to tax the strength of the cyclist undertaking it. Thus to A, whose spins rarely exceed fifty miles, a century may fairly ) be styled a long-distance performance, demanding as much effort to successfully accomplish as a jaunt Z. of 200 miles undertaken by 13, whose week-end mileage often runs in'to three figures. The slightest reflection will suggest to the expe- rienced cyclist the importance of starting fora long ride on a sound, light machine, fitted with a saddle ): known to be comfortable. The precaution should .. be observed of examining the tyres, and the comfort of the rider's dress may be increased by wearing eav shoes, cellular shirt, and a light cap. No forcing of the pace at the Start should be indulged in at the expense of undue effort, or a corresponding fit of the slows will be the consequence. 3, As a long-distance ride will necessarily take the cyclist over country of extremely varied character, , the cycle should, of course, be equipped for easily ' surmounting all difficulties.. If one of the well- known coaster hubs, like the New Departure, the Radie boaster Hub, or the Cr. & H. Coaster Hub, is built into the cycle, there need only be fitted one rim-brake, and that on the frent rim, for emergency Purposes. If, however, the cycle is of the two- speed variety, fitted with a Hub Two-Speed Gear or a Fagan Two-Speed Hub for easy hill climbing, then the rider should look to it that he has a brake on both front and back wheels, and the Bowden or Vim varieties are quite impossible to beat. Most important of all, however, is the manage- ment of the rider's feeding. Food must be taken frequently and in small quantities.. On no account Should a heavy meal be indulged in until after the finish of the daY's ride. If an unusually early start be made, it will be necessary for the rider to carry such refreshment as he may need until the ordinary houses shall be open. For example; the following is an outline of the feeding, which experience has proved to be best, in the case of a hundred miles ride carried out by ordinary club men: Before the start, take the ordi- nary breakfast; after twenty-five miles, a drink of ginger-beer or soda-and-milk, with piece of cake or clry biscuits; atforty-three miles, a similar drink; at fifty-six miles, lunch—a light but satisfying meal Of cold meat, salad, milk-pudding, and stewed fruit, With a cool drink of limejnice-and-soda or bock- and-socla ; at seventy miles, a cup of tea and slice or two of bread-and-butter; at ninety miles, tea, . with boiled eggs. lettuce, Bz.e. ; the remaining dis- tance to be ridden without a stop. ' ' Although to the inexperienced the number of stops for refreshment may appear excessive and Wasteful of .thne, it must be borne in mind that these halts serve to break up the ride into sections of lengths quite familiar to the rider, while the refreshment staves off the collapse liable to occur as the day wears on about three hours after a meal. Much of the distress experienced on long journeys la due to want of nourishment, for the appetite fre- quently fails to warn the rider of his bodily needs Until exhaustion has ippeared.

Leaky Valves Whether a cycle is in constant use or not, it is tlcsirable that the tyre valve-rubbers should be ezmnined every few`months. If there be any sign wear at the shoulder of the movable spindle or Plug, the rubber should be removed and a new one "Illastituted. This precaution will not only save trouble from leaky valves, but will keep the. valves in efficient working order should it be necessary to effate a tyre when repairing a puncture. Nothing 's More annoying than to have a valve-stem stick when engaged in a roadside repair, as, is often the ease if the rubber has perished. In the effort to Withdraw the plug, the ,rubber sleeve, when old, is inclined to adhere to fife body of the valve. and 'is

A Club Gazette.

For many years past the vocabulary of the cycling public has been widened and enriched by that lively club organ, "The Bath Road News.' The contributors to its pages include, perhaps, the hardest riders who also wield a pen. Their breezy narratives of adventures on the road abound with stories of "passing it through" those whom they encounter awheel, while "acid eating" describes the suffering they endure in their self-imposed taslts. A bicycle becomes a "speed iron" or a "crock." An impromptu speed struggle is a "scrap," and a " snag " refers to any obstruction or hindrance met with when in full career. Speedy opponents are "hot stuff," while a worn out rider wears a " knackered " appearance. Ablutions before a meal are tersely styled a "swab." Selections from the lurid phraseology ' of the Bath Roaders from time to time find their way into the public journals of the pastime, and thus become current amongst enthusiasts of the wheel outside the famous club

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

DEATH OF A WIDNES MEDICAL OFFICFR.

Dr. Urquhart M`Lennan, medical officer of health for the borough of Widnes, passed peacefully away at his residence, Highfleld-road, Widnes, at a quarter ter eleven on Sunday morning. The deceased gentleman had been ailing for a long time past, and it was only a month ago that he received a comforting welcome from the members of the Council upon the resumption of his duties, although far from convalescent. On Monday be was present at the opening of the transporter bridge. On Friday the deceased gentleman took to his bed after Monday's strain upon his constitution. He leaves a widow and family and a wide circle of friends to mourn his loss. The late Dr. M'Lennan started busi- ness about twenty-five years ago in Widnes, and fbr the last ten years has been the town's medical adviser. It has been due to his assiduous attention to duty that the borough of Widnes attained the splendid distinction of being one of the healthiest towns in the kingdom.

AN ECHO OF THE TEST MATCH.

Very great interest has been excited in Liverpool by the test match and as usual the scores have been exhibited outside Mr. Frank Sugg's the old Lancashire County Batsman, Athletic Outfitting Establishment in Lord-street. Repeatedly during past years the police have taken proceedings against Mr. Sugg for causing a crowd to assemble. The summonses have been treated by the different magistrates in different ways. The stipendnry on one occasion adjourned tho case sine die, expressing the opinion that the telegrams were a convenience to public and that no one ought to complain if momentarily their path was obstructed. Last year however, the magistrates convicted in one instance and inflicted a nominal fine on the grounds that the telegrams were exhibited ohtside the building line and therefore on the thoroughfare, and on that occasion though the magistrates were invited to express an opinion, they declined to say whether or not they would convict, even supposing the telegtams had been inside the window. _ _

Two summonses were issued last week,one against Mr. Frank Sugg and one against Mr. Bailey, manager. The defendants argued that as the numbers were given in a large form within the building line on the second floor, they were not legally responsible for the crowds that assembled. A considerable discussion took place between the prosecuting solicitor, the bench, and Mr Sanders the chief clerk. The police evidence was to the effect that the Tramway was obstructed as well as the footpath, though the police admitted that the rest of the tradesmen in Lord street with a commendable sporting instinct refused to give any evidence of incon- venience or obstryction. The magistrates dismissed both summonses though at the request of the prosecu- ting solicitor, they agreed to state a case for appeal. Mr. Sanders the Magistrates' clerk held that the Bench had no jurisdiction and that the defendant could, if the police saw fit, be indicted at the Assizes. The prose- cuting solicitor said this step would be taken if necessary.

GLE ANINGS.

PROF lISSOR RUTHERFORD, F.R.S., has found by ex-peritnent that the heating effect of radium is largely, due to the bombardment of the alpha particles expelled from its mass.

Mn. BosiomEn, of the Australian telegraph service, has traced small perforations in the lead covering of aerial telephone cables to the larvie of the Bostrycus jesuitica, a little black insect, and to the Zylopertha, a common insect in Australia.

A HEAVY RAINFALL.

The heaviest rainfall of *lion duration ever recorded accurately is said to have occurred at Santiago de Cuba. An English engineer found the rate to be more than four inches an hour, and between seven and ten o'clock in the evening a total fall of more than a foot was registered by the gauge. The storm covered nearly 300 square miles, in places being even heavier than at the point where the measurement was made.

===== 0002601_19050610_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
LF,CCITE.

Leucite is a felspar geologically, and a double silicate of alumina and potash chemically, found certain lavas, notably of Vesuvius and the Roman Campagna, where its grey geometrical crystals attract attention. The Societa Romana Solfati of Rome collects leucite between Frascati and Albano, and between Rome and Naples, to manufacture alum from it. Other magnetic minerals found with it are removed by electro- magnets. Sulphuric' acid applied to the leucite yields alum, but in order to purify it the crude alum is dissolved and crystallised.

===== 0002601_19050610_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTRICITY FOR FRACTURES.

An Italian scientist, Francesco Blasi, has been experimenting on the influence of various electric currents on the knitting of bones. The action of 'galvanic, static, and faradic currents was tried on fractured bones of rabbits. Although all the currents showed some beneficial effects, the galvanic current was by all means the most effective; the swelling was quickly reduced, and the union of the bone hastened. After the helpful action of galvanic current had been demonstrated on rabbits, their influence was tried on man. The same beneficial results were obtained. In one case where the bone had refused to knit for over a month galvanism was applied, and in three weeks the fracture was cured. In cases treated by galvanism there is a noticeable lessening of the usual atrophy and inability to use the part after the removal of the splint.

===== 0002601_19050610_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
AN OBJECTION TO SEA SPRAY.

The spray of the sea which so many delight to find blowing in their faces may not be the health- bringing agent generally imagined. A foreign correspondent of the Lancet, M. Busquet, has been studying the part which such spray can play in the transmission of disease. It can be carried inland at least for 50 metres, and it also is carried high into the air, so that it will fill with mist houses which are close to the water's edge. On the sea front of one place investigated, where numerous sewers discharge, given a strong wind with thick spray, the usual number of germs in the air is trebled. With a gale blowing the proportion of germs is increased in a still higher ratio, and virulent bacilli coil have been found. These researches sliew the necessity for the protection of shores from pollution by sewage, and incidentally destroy yet another of our ideals.

===== 0002601_19050610_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HUDSON RIVER CANYON.

Investigations of the sea bottom of the Atlantic at distances of a hundred miles and more off New York harbour have shewn that part of the old bed of the Hudson—now submerged—was at the bottom of a great canyon. At one period in the geological history of the American continent the eastern coast was some 9,000 ft. higher than now, and in conse- quence the Atlantic Ocean was many miles from the present site of New York. The Hudson River on its way to the sea appears to have had for a part of the way a canyon at least 3,8001 t. deep, and with a width of less than two miles. The soundings have not been carried out systematically enough as yet to enable geologists to map all of the topographical features of this ancient river bed, but it is known that nearer its mouth the. canyon spread ont into a widening valley which probably ran down to the sea.

===== 0002601_19050610_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
COLD WATER AND POISON.

In connection with the subject of water, there is one peculiar property of that liquid with which everyone should be made acquainted, and that is its capacity for absorbing impurities, which increases proportionately the colder it gets. Hence, water that has stood in an insufficiently ventilated sleeping, chamber all night is not only unpleasant, but positively injurious to drink, since it readily absorbs the poisonous gases given off by respiration and the action of the skin. An ordinary pitcher of water, under such conditions, at a tqmperature of 60deg., will be found to have absorbed during the night from a pint to a pint and a half of carbonic acid gas, and an increasa of ammonia. Ice water is an objectionable drink at all times, but if it is indulged in the vessel con- taining it should never be left uncovered in sleep- ing or sitting-rooms, because at freezing-point its capacity for absorbing these deleterious substances is nearly doubled.

===== 0002601_19050610_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WEIGHT OF CROWDS.

In "Molesworth's Pocket-Book" we find it stated that, for rough calculations of the loads on bridges, 1201 b. per square foot may be taken for the weight of a crowd of people; while in "Traut- wine's Pocket-Book we are told that a suspension bridge should not have a factor of safety of less than three against a load of people, whose weight is taken at 100 lb. per square foot, which includes an allowance for wind, and for the crowd being in motion. Many years ago, the engineers of the Chelsea Bridge had a number of picked men placed on the table of a weighing machine, and packed together as closely as possible. It was then found that a load on the table of 841 b. per square foot was produced. Professor L. J. Johnson, of Harvard University, has shewn, however, says Enyineering, that under certain conditions it is possible to obtain much higher figures than those we have mentioned, for, he has carried out experiments which shew that a loading of 150Ib. to 160Ib. per square foot may be produced by the weight of an assembled group of persons without undue crowd- ing; and ,later experiments on similar lines shewed that with a maximum condition of loading, or one in which forty picked men were placed on an area of 36 square feet, a load of 181.31 b. per square foot was obtained. This is, of course, an extreme figure, and could only be obtained with picked men; and Professor Johnson states that they were all tall and carefully selected. The men all faced in one direction, a position which favours close packing; but it is a condition of things that might easily occur naturally in a crowd.

===== 0002601_19050610_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
KILLING INSECTS BY ELECTRICITY.

If criminals and other undesirables are killed by the aid of electricity, it should also be possible, according to the Electrical Magazine, to mete out a like fate to all those insects which, to the annoy- ance and loss of the farmer, take up their abode in the upper strata of the subsoil. Though, perhaps, no one will deny the theoretical possibility of electrocuting a beetle or grasshopper, still, perhaps, he will wonder why electricians have not already joined hands with the farmer for putting this valuable ally in his possession. The bodies of insects, and especially of crustaceans, present, relatively speaking, a high electrical resistance, and wet earth is a good conductor of electricity. It is thus obvious that a current of electricity, directed into damp soil, will do but little harm to insects dwelling therein. Mr. Helberger, engineer, of Monaco. on working in an open field with electrical machinery, found, on sinking into the ground metal rods connected to a 110-volt dynamo, that all the insects came out into the open air almost directly the current had been passed through the earth. In other experi- ments an ordinary dynamo is mounted on a stout truck, and is driven by gearing from the wheels of the vehicle itself, so that it does not generate electricity unless the vehicle is in motion. The current generated is conducted to earth by the metal wheels on the one side and on the other to a series of copper-tipped brushes mounted on the rear portion of the cart, and so disposed as to keep their tips a few inches from the surface of the soil without coming into contact with it. Between the electrified earth and the brushes a continuous shower ldiksparks took place, which caused all the unfortunate inhabitants of the upper strata of the earth to meet with as instantaneous a death as if they had been struck by lightning. It will be nhvious that if the treatment tie sufhcientiv intense the insects must be utterly aestroyed, but that the =aunt of current necessary to effect the operation would probably be so high with this systera as to render it hardly economical.

The English Sunday Closing Bill.

Sir,—lt is remarkable to note the numberless varia- tions of opinions and ideas expressed on any stihject by various people at different times. Of course it is not quite so remarkable when all the facts are considered, nevertheless it would be extremely interesting to watch, if we could,the operations of the mind, with the external influences acting upon it, when and while cer- tain conclusions are being arrived at on a fixed question Why is it we come to hold such opinions, why is it that certain evidences lead one person to one conclusion and another person to an entirely opposite one ?Why do some people believe in the existence of God and hold it quite logical and rational to so believe. Yet others prob- ably quite as sane cannot for the life of them see how such a belief can be logically entertained. And o with myself—l cannot possibly comprehend how "Tone "links God with the Sunday closing bill in the manner that he does. Such languageas this for instance —"whenever any attempt is made in Parliament to up- hold the honour of God"—is really absurd and extremely loose. When my friend wrote down the word " God ' had he any adequate idea of what he meant, did he mean anything at all, or was it merely a catch word used because popular, but to which only a vague and rusty meaning attaches. But to speak of the "honour of God" is, without doubt, amusing. What conception has our friend of the honour of God? Honour as applied to God is ridiculous. There can really be no question of honour where an omnipotent God is concerned. Verily it has come to a pretty pass when God's honour has to be upheld and defended and the very reason for it, as far as I can see, is the fact that "lona' evidently thinks God has planned certain things to happen but, somehow or other, matters have gone wrong and sin has entered into the world audit is there- fore necessary to argue that God not having originally in His scheme of things placed sin in the catalogue of events we must not now dishonour Him by ascribing evil as one of His productions. God is not responsible for and is not the author inanyshaee or form of certain acts and events that have occurred in this world—this is what "Ions" would say I presume.To say that he is the author is to dishonour' Him—is this "Cona's " meaning? It appears to me that some people know more about God than God knows about Himself. We ape Jr of the laws of God as though we had helped God to make them at some specially convened committee meeting, or as though some text book existed wherein is set in black and white all the laws of the universe. My frieud may know a lot about God and His Will; he may think he does, but for my part God is so far removed from the bricks and morta4and such like of this world that He appears not to exit, and until I can claim a minister's acquaintance with God and His wonderful ways, I must remain spiritually blind, and beg a fewoppers of grace from more richly endowed brethren. To say that God is not responsible is a grave statement. Is there any- thing a God cannot be responsible for? And then my friend speaks of Mr. Balfour in connection with his laxity in not reminding his hearers in Parlia- ment that there is a law of God read in the churches every Sunday which prohibits work of all kinds from being done on the seventh day. Now Mr Balfour is not troubled with any great belief in God at all, and certaialy Mr. Balfour does not believe in the rubbish concerning the law of God and the seventh day. Mr. Balfour, like other sensible people, wants to know when and where did God make such a law, and how do we know that He did make a law at all ? And what a lot of fuss some Christians make over the drink question. The soul is not physical, why then should beer which only has physical results injure it in any way. What is there in beer that has such a power- ful influence in the soul of man. It seems rather strange that the soul of man should be so mightily dis- turbed by mere liquor. Restriction of man's liberty is no remedy of the drink question. While things are as they are men and women will drink. It is sad, perhaps, but nevertheless true. When man's soul and his God are dispensed with and common sense begins to appear then will i be time to talk of effectively solving the drink problem.— Tours &c,

"ORTHODOX."

Mr. Chamberlain at St. Helens. Attack on Labour Representatives. Sir,—ln reading the reports of Mr. Chamberlain's speeches in St. Helens, and especially that portion in which he refers to the Labour Representatives, one be- comes more than ever convinced that Mr. Chamberlain is entirely out of touch with present day labour politics, or that he is feigning ignorance. With your kind permission I will here insert that portion of the report to which I refer, so that your readers may have an opportunity of becoming acquainted with Mr. Chamberlain's views of our representatives. He is reported to have said " I believe that I have behind me the mass of working people. (Hear, hear.) . . Oh, but,' it is said, 'have you considered the representatives of labour are against you ?' Who are the representatives of labour ? (Cheers.) Whom do you mean? Do you mean those who call themselves by that name and who sit in Parliament with the assist- ance of a political party, the men who are most excel- lent party politicians and very often very bad labour representatives? Did those men represent you at the time of the Boer War? No. Every man was against us, against the war. Every man desired we should have surrendered, given up our position in the world,lost the confidence of our colonies rather than continue the war. Did they represent you in regard to Home Rule? They represented some, no doubt. Did they represent you when the Compensation Act was before Parliament ? Can any of you tell me of one of them who gave me any assistance ? Did they represent you in regard to the Aliens Bill ? (No.) Well, what do they represent? (Cheers.) I appeal from them, if necessary. I do nots think, after all,they are so numerous as they themselve suppose—but I appeal from them to those who made them—to the great democracy of this country, who on all those points I have referred to were instinctively right, instinctively patriotic, instinctivly prudent and wise. (Cheers.)" Mr. Chamberlain must have a powerful imagination when he can believe the mass of the working people are behind him. Almost everything that has happened recently in political life points the contrary, and proves that the mass, or to be more accurate the large majority .of the working class is against his proposals. Certainly the mass of organized labour is against him. The Trades Union Congress, General Federation of Trades Unions, and the Labour Representation Committee.have at their annual Conferences, almost unanimously condemned his proposals. Not a single trade union, Trades Council, or Trades Council Federation of any standing, has ap- proved, by resolution, of Mr. Chamberlain's doctrine. In his speech he comdemns the Labour M.P.'s as "most excellent party politicians and very often very bad labour• representatives." One is bound to confess that! there is a good deal of truth in that statement, when applied to the section which is attached to the Liberal Party. These men however have done good work on behalf of Labour on very many occasions. The state- ment can in no way be applied to those members of Parliament who are associated with the Labour Repre- sentation Committee. Messrs. Crooks, Henderson, -Shackletoo, Heir Hardie, and others cannot by any stretch of imagination be truthfully called "party politicians before labour representatives." If Mr. Chamberlain has not yet learnt the difference between the two Labour forces at present in the House .of Commons, a most important part of his education ,has been neglected. A-Most significant fact, however, and one to which I invite your readers attention, is that both the old and the new school of Labour Representa- tives, are solidly united in their opposition to the proposed alterations in our fiscal system. The fact of this uunanimity against Mr. Chamberlain's proposals, may be the reason why be can see no difference between the two sections of the Labour movement. He condems the Labour Representatives because they opposed the South African War; they support Home Rule ; opposed the Alien's Bill, and gave him no assistance when the Compensation Act was before Parliament. If these acts of omission and commission are crimes in the eves of Mr. Chamberlain and his friends, the rank and file of the Labour movement think otherwise. We are proud of our opposition to the War, which,by the way,was to he a "Miners' War,"—a war which cost us £250,000,000 and :0,000 British lives, in which this country was swindled wholesale by many of the contractors, a war which made desolate two hitherto flourishing colonies. What for? To make it possible for the millionaires and their friends, to secure Chinese coolies and use them to " blackleg " the Katfirs, who refused to submit to the great reductions in wages which took place at the close of the war. We now gather from press reports that there is a great difficulty in securing Chinese laboer,and that the few which are being secured,are of the criminal class. One does not dare to imagine what South Africa will be like if this crime is permitted to conttaue. It is quite true our representatives supported Home _Rule for Ireland, and in doing so have our approval. No self-respecting Labour representatives could Le satisfied with the Compensation Act which leaves such a large number of working men out of its provisions, which does not provide for compensation for the first fortnight, and which is so difficult tc interpret that the class receiving the largest compensation under its pro- visions is the legal fraternity. Another question referred to by Mr. Chamberlain is the Aliens' Bill. We condemn it as a bogus attempt to deal with a question which is of very Little national importance. I don't think any Labour cepresentative would object to a Bill which would " prevent the immigration of diseased or "criminal" aliens. We strongly object however to any attempt to prevent an alien trade unionist or Socialist from taking refuge in this country from the oppression of a tyrannical Con- tinental monarch or Government. Will Mr. Chamber- -lain insert, or cause to be inserted, a clause in the Bill which would prevent workmen aliens being brought , here to take the places of men on strike, or locked out I and AISO to make it impossible for any alien workmen I

to be employed in this country at less than the Iritish trade union rates of wages? Mr. Chamberlain asks "Well, what do they repre- akt ?" they of course being the Labour representatives. If I may, as one of the rank and file, answer that ques- tion, my reply is, that the Labour Representatives repre- sent that new spirit which is growing in the Labour movement, and which will not be satisfied with our present system of Free Trade, nor the Retaliation or Protection proposed by the Balfour-Chamberlain com- pact. They represent a party which is not so much anxious for more emnloyment as for a more equal dis- tribution of the wealth created. They represent a party which desires security for Trade Union funds; old-age pensions ; a shorter workingday ; to remove the burden of landlordism ; the oppressive weight of mining rents ,and royalties ' - excessive railway rates; to provide work for the unemployed ; the demolition 'of slums; more equal system df taxation; a real national system of education, and other reforms. These will not be secured by folio wing Mr. Chariaberlain and his friends, who have "ever promised but never fulfilled." These reforms can be secured by building up a strong and united labour party, independent of both the great political parties, but willing to co-operate with, or oppose either, as cir- cumstances demand. 160, Battersby-lane, Warrington.

FRED STDTT.

'June 8, 1906..

Charge Against a Famous Footballer. Runcorn Police Court was a busy place on Monday morning, when the Bench heard no less than 28 summonses against individuals for 'betting. —Samuel Houghton, of the Egerton Arms Hotel, and the popular Rugby full-back, was charged with unlawfully allow- ing a game of dominoes on his premises on May 13. A s6mewhat heated discussion on the proposal to approach Mr. Carnegie with a view to obtaining a grant for library extension purposes took place at the meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday evening.
