RELIGIOUS EDUCATION.

Rev. H. Dunnico's Reply to the Rev. Morley Stevenson.

The Baptist Church was crowded on Sunday evening last when the Rev. H. Duanico delivered a sermon in reply to that recently delivered by thq Rev. Morley Stevenson on Education. Basing his remarks on the words,"He that ruleth over men must be just "(2 Samuel 3.), the preacher said that in reply to several requests he had undertaken the task of replying te Mr. Stevenson. He made no apology for preaching on the subject and did net sea why Mr. Stevenson should do so. If a man did what he believed to be his duty why did he apologise for so doing? Was it so unusual a thing to preach a "sort of political" sermon in an Anglican pulpit? He did not think so. He (the preacher) had spent twelve or thirteen years in the fold of the Anglican Church and it was there he learned his first lesson in that art. He was willing to admit that he had advanced since then. In those days he had been taught that political sermons were only permissible when the interests of the Church" were in danger. Now he knew that political sermons were permissible whenever the interests Of truth were at stake or the welfare of humanity was in danger. Mr. Duunico then said that he was pleaded wit n the general tone of Mr. Stevenson's address. It was

MUCH MORE TOLERANT than that they (Free Churchmen) were accustomed to hear from such quarters. Still the whole address was misleading and was conspicuous for what it omitted. He deeply regretted this. Mr. Stevenson was the Principal of a Training College: he was looked upon as an authority on Educational matters; his words natur- ally would carry weight in some quarters and it was his duty to have made sure of his facts and not to have given utterance to statements that no careful student of education would have given utterance to. Proceeding to quota from Mr. Stsvenson's address the preacher dealt with several statements. I Mr. Stevenson had admitted that "noble help had been rendered by other religious bodies in matters of educa- tion " and that relieved him from discussing the respec- tive claims of these bodies. That was a question 'ae (the preacher) would deal with on a future occasion. The first statement, however, Mr. Dunnico tonk excep- tion to was the one to the effect that 200 years ago the people of this country were uneducated, whereupon the Church said "This is not right. The people ought to be educated." In 1698 the Society for the Promotion of Christian Knowledge was formed, and soon covered a large part of the country with schools in which the people of the country were taught without money and without price. Mr. Btevenson had gone on to say that the work of this Society became so large that 100 years later another Society was formed, called the National Society, for thepurpose of taking over the work. This Society, according to Mr. Stevenson, was founded in 1804. All this, Mr. Donnie° contended, was in the highest degree misleading. Mr. Stevenson knew per- fectly well, if he did not he ought to, that the S.P.C.K. was a religious institution, and had little if any con- nection with a system of elementary education as we understood that phrase to-day. The

===== 0002601_19050610_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
HISTORY: OF ELF.MIthARY EDUCATION

in the modern and proper meaning commenced in the closing, years of the 18th and the early years of the 19th centuries./ It was quite true that large sums of money had been left for the purpose of educating the poor, but the great bulk of the money had been misappropriated and was used for educating the children of the wealthier classes. The report of the commission of 1818 demon- strated the accuracy of his (the preacher's) contention. IThe working of the S.P.C.K. had nothing what- ever to do with elementary education. It was unfair to suggest that it had, and that particular ques- tion could be dismissed as being outside the present controversy. Mr. Stevenson had stated that the reason for found- ing the National Society in 1801 was for the purpose of taking over the work of the S.P.C.K., which Society I had according to Mr. Stevenson covered a large part of the country with free schools. This again was not in accordance with facts. In the first place the National Society was not founded in 1804. The first annual report bore the date August sth, 1812, and the Society was founded in 1311 not 1804. This was perhaps only a Iminor matter in the opinion of some but it proved that 1 Mr. Stevenson's statements might be more accurate. In the second place he would like Mr. Stevenson to tell them how was it that, if the S.P.C.K. covered a large part of the country with schools, it was a very rare occurrence to find a labouring man or woman who could read as late as 1800? The answer was that such schools existed only in the too fertile imagination of the preacher. In the third place it was untrue to say that the National Society was founded to take over the work of the S.P C.K.liis(the preacher's)hearers were no doubt aware that it was a young Quaker, Joseph Lancaster who FIRST TOOK UP POPULAR I:DUCATION

as understood to-thy and that his work was immedi- ately successful. This success roused the hostility and jealousy of the Church. Dr. Bell the founder of the National Society himself said "that thousands in various parts of the country were being drawn from the Church by the superinr attention paid to education outside the church." The real reasons for forming the National Society had been concisely stated by Lord John Russel a staunch churchman. He said "In 1807 the British and Foreign School Society commenced its work of providing schools for all. The Established Church pronounced at first the opinion that the children of the poor should not go to school at all. Finding however that George 111. had expressed a wish that every child in his dominion should be able to read the Bible and that he along with the Dukes of Kent, Sussex and Bedford were ardent supporters of Lancaster's schools, also that public opinion was favourable to education,it changed its ground and set up the so-called National Society on a narrow and exclusive principle." Lancaster advocated and carried out in his system the sound principle that national education ought not to be made subservient to the propagation of the peculiar tenets of any religious denomination. The British Society was founded on these broad and Catholic lines. TIIE NATIONAL SOCIETY . was founded in opposition to this its founders contend- ing that if any education was to be given to the poor the Church alone had the right to give it. It might seem incredible but it was true that Dr. Bell the originator of the " National Society " was opposed to the poor being taught either to write or cipher. He contended that the diffusion of general knowledge among the poor was " utopean " and calcu- lated to confuse the distinction of ranks, and classes of society and to make the labourer unhappy and discon- tented with his lot. He (111r.Dannico) would leave his hearers to judge why and for what purpose the National Society was formed. The Anglican Church had not lifted hand or foot in the interests of education except for clerical ends. At first hostile to the instruction of the masses in any form they found it necessary at last to take it up in order to protect and preserve their own privileges and emoluments. Mr. Stevenson had stated that the Church's work for many years was to RoCiE TICE CONSCIENCE OF VIE STATE in the matter of education. That also Iva', not true. In 1807 the House of Commons passed a Bill enabling the rate-payers to set up a school in each parish but when the measure went before tho House of Lords the Arch- I Bishop of Canterbury warmly opposed it supported by 1 his Bishops and the " Lords " rejected it. Did this look like the Church rousing the conscience of the State? Such a contention was ridiculous. Then Mr. Stevenson had asserted that the first grant ever given by the State for educational purposes was in 1833. Surely he was aware that that there was a period of English history known as the Commonwealth period. Among many distinctions of that period it made popular education among. its chief concerns and made a large grant each year in support of it. Those grants preceded by is I veers the date given by Mr. Stevenson as the date of the first grant and was another testimony to the accurracy of that gentleman's statements. This was also the only period in English history that the Roman or Anglic in Church had not been predominant and yet for- sooth we were told the "Conscience of the state was asieep"and the work of the Anglican church was to rouse it. After a brief reference to the Act of 1870.Mr.Dunnico took up the question of tho cost of the schools. Mr. Stevenson contended that THE CIIURCII PEOI'LE OF WARRINGTON

He doubted whether they got anything cheaper than their com- petitors, taking into account the relative amount of living as in America, for instance, and if they did get things at a lower price they had less money to purchase them with.

COLONIAL RELATIONS.

The second branch of their policy, which was more urgent and important than the first, was the question of preferential relations with our colonies. (Applause). While our trade with the foreigner had been decreasing all this time, both in quantity and character, exactly the reverse had been the case with our Colonies. There were now only eleven millions of white'people in those colonies. Would they allow their imaginations to run loose as to what their trade might be with these Colonies when the population was fifty millions or more. There was no limit to the extent to which the Homeland and her Colonies could contri- bute to each other's prosperity, and their Imperial trade was the greatest asset of the Imperial dominion. This Empire could well be self-sustained, and it would be a satisfaction to every patriot that whilst he was benefit- ing himself he was also helping to benefit s fellow citizen elsewhere.

THE PARTY SYSTENI. He would have expected that the people of this country unanimously would have called for a Confer- ence. Let us know what our friends and brothers wished from us, and we would try if it were not possible without some inconceivable sacrifice to meet them. (Cheers.) But for our party system he thought this would have been done. Our party system was good enough in many cases, but in a great national crisis like this it sadly broke down. He assured the country that it was false to suppose that such an arrangement with the Colonies would necessitate a tax on food, though it might entail the transfer of taxation from one kind of food to another kind of food. Under the worst possible circurnstanees it could not possibly mean more than a farthing on the quartern loaf.which would probably add nothing at all and would certainly reduce other portions of the expenditure of the working man. They turned great questions of national existence into purely party weapons. Speaking on the Imperial considerations attaching to the question, he said, could they shut their eyes to the actual conditions of this gloat Empire ? It was not an Empire in the true sense of the word, but a bundle of sticks bound together by a very thin cord, a very strong cord if they liked, a cord of sympithy and sentiment, but hardly strong enough to meet all the contingencies of the future. (Cheers.) The Colonies no longer belonged to us ; they were sister States,which we could bind to ourselves or neglect or snub or insult, as some of our statesmen seemed inclined to do. Separation would be disaster for us, if not less for the Colonies. Let us unite, and we might stand square against all the winds that blow. Let us sepamte,and there was an end, a close, of the great chapter of British history and British civilization. (Cheers.) THE EBB TIDE.

What their opponents had done might delay, but it would not prevent the fruition of their policy. Self- I interest, common sense, and patriotism were on their side. Their cause must be victorious. (Loud cheers). Why did he say" must " ? It was because, by our part I system, we must always reckon on an ebb and flow in popular opinion. The tide was flowing at the present moment, he did not deny, very strongly in favour of their opponents. But the ebb would coine—(loud cheers)—and in the interval before the next reaction their opponents might secure the offices for which they had made so many sacrifices of parliamentory courtesy and ordinary good manners. (Laughter and cheers). ff their opponents came into officehe was perfectly cer- tain that after a few months' experience the 'country would be much more anxious to get rid of them. than they were to put them in. (Laughter and cheers). Then the Conservatives had in the meantime a clear and definite policy. They had a policy in which they were united—a policy approved by their leader, and accepted by a vast majority of the party, and when this inevitable ebb came they should get home again, and they should bri3Og in their trail their policy behind them' . (Cheers.) He had only one condition, and that was that they must have such a policy—a fighting policy, supported by the enthusiasm of the party. In politics, as in war, they could not win on the defensive and he thought their recent misfortunes bad been partly caused by the want of this fighting and constructive policy. A TAX ON CORN.

The previous night there was a great and impressive meeting to pay a well-deserved honour to the Prime Minister, who had earned the respect of all and the devoted affection of his own followers. It was his business on that occasion to review the situation,and to look into the future, and every loyal Unionist would be

LOCAL MINISTER'S SUDDEN DEATH.

The business of the Presbytery of Manchester, which was held in the Grosvenor Square Presbyterian Church on Monday afternoon, was brought to a sudden end by the death of the Rev. W. Dunn. He only retirad from the ministry of Risley Church a weeks agoland had removed to Whalley Range. He attended the Presbytery on Monday because he wanted to take part in the discussion respecting his old church, and he made a vigorous speech in favour of its being continued a sanction charge. At the close of the gathering the members adjourned, as usual, for tea. In the tea-ro.an Mr. Dunn fell down in a sudden illness. A messenger went to a neighbouring surgery for Dr. Johnson, and although that gentleman attended immediately he could only say that death had already taken place. Mr. Dunn during the afternoon had given no sign of illness. He was nearly eighty years of age. Risley Church, of which Mr. Dunn had been minister for nearly twenty years, was built about 1660, and is known as a " five-mile church," it having been built when Nonconformists were not allowed to build chinches or hold services within five miles of a corporate town. Risley Church is about five miles from Warrington. The deceased minister was very well-known to the Presbyterians 'of Warrington and district and had on many occasions addressed the annual congregational gatherings in connection with St. John's Church, Winwick street.

===== 0002601_19050610_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INQUEST.

The Manchester Deputy Coroner (Mr. %V. Sellars) on Tuesday inquired into the death of the Rev. W. Dunn. His son stated that for 20 years his father had been troubled with heart disease.—The Rev. E. H. Gray, of High-street, Chorlton-on-Medlock, who said he had known Mr. Dunn for eight years, explained that on Monday afternoo.i a meeting of the Presbytery was held at the Presbyterian Church, Grosvenor-square, Manchester,and though lately his friends had persuaded him not to give addresses Mr. Dunn seemed anxious to speak. He spoke with some vigour, and appeared ex- cited. After the meeting an adjournment was made to the tearoom, where Mr. Dunn fell to the floor and died. A N erdict of death from natural causes was returned.

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. The Council and Furious Motoring.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Raral Di 3 trict Council Wa9 held at Bewsey Chambers on Wednes- day when there were present :—Dr. Murray (presiding), MeAsrs. G. Ball, G. Mather, W. Longton, J. W. Knowles, J. Backhouse, S. Sutton (assistant clerk), B. S. Newcombe (surveyor), H. H. Burnage (assistant surveyor), F. W. Kerfoot (inspector), and Dr. Sephton (medical officer).

===== 0002601_19050610_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
THANKS.

At the outset Dr. Nit'EtßkY returned thanks for his election s-s chairman. He hoped he would deserve their confidence and retain their esteem during his term of office.

FLAGGING AT PENKETII.

A resolution was read from the Periketh Parish meet- ing asking the Council to endeavour to induce the Lancashire County Council to flag both footpaths in the district running from the Red Lion Inn to the Co-opera- tive Stores. It was decided that the Surveyor shoed interview the distirct surveyor on the:matter. - INCREASED DEATH RATE. . . .

Dr. SEPIITON. reported that daring the month there had been 27 births and 53 deaths. The death-mte was equal to 573 Or 1,000, as against 34 the average of the past few, months, and 358 for the corresponding period of last year. Thirty-six deaths occurred at the County Asylum, Winwick, exclusive of us the death-fate was 18.3 per 1,000. There had been an outbreak of whoop- ing cough at Penketh, and he had had the infant school closed for a fortnight. THE SPEED OF MOTOR CARS. Mr. TROMPSON asked if anything was being done with regard to the motor question. The nuisance was be- coming general, and property-Owners and farmers were all complaining about it. Mr. KNOWLES suggested that the Council should write to the superintendent of police pointing out the excessive speed of motor cars and the danger thereby caused to the public. A resolution to this effect was moved by Mr. THOMPSON. The SURVEYOR said that with reference to the dust caused by motor cars preventatives had not a lasting effect. He thought, however, something could be done to lesson the cause of complaint by using tarred copper slag on the roads. The Surveyor was instructed to ascertain the cost of copper slag.

===== 0002601_19050610_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
grateful for the lead which he was able to give them. They had now from his lips a clear exposition of official policy for the next election,which he did not think 'yen Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman would be able to mis- understand. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Balfour said Tiff Reform will be the most important part of the Unionist policy—(applause)—and that Colonial preference was the most important part of tariff reform. (Hear, hear.) Therefore, they would be in the Unionist programme. He also asked all of them through that great audience that this question, which, as he truly said, touched the whole Empire, should be referrei ton conference repre- senting the whole Empire, and that that conference should be absolutely free, and that all concerned should afterwards be free to considerand deal with the results, whatever they might be. There was an official pro- gramme to which he (Kr. Chamberlain) most heartily subscribed. (Great applause.) It was a clear and unmistakable one, and great amongst its merits was that it was entirely and absolutely opposed to the policy of the Radicals, who would rather sacrifice the union—nay, the Empire itself—than subscribe to any- thing which involved a taxation of corn. He pointed out that in his view it would be i.npossible to provide for a preference which the Colonies had it in their power to offer them, and bethought they would offer, unless they were willing to put some small duty on corn which came from foreign countries, not in order to raise the price of corn' which he did not think it would do, but in order to transfer the trade in corn from foreign lands to British soil. (Applause.) They must make the transfer in such a way as not to injure the poorest in this country. That was his own firm belief, but at least he asked his friends to wait and see the results of the conference. On the other hand let no man commit himself rashly to the other side ot the question till the whole matter was before him. This policy of Mr. Balfour could surely be accepted by every loyal Unionist who was not a bigoted Cohdenite. Of such men there must be a few in the country. If the introduction of this policy was such as to force them to withdraw themselves from the party he should deeply regret it, but he would think it much better than that they should remain as a drag upon the wheels.^ Let, then, the vast majority of the party rally to the flag that Mr. Balfour had raised, and with the party consolidated and with a great issue to fight for, they would carry the standard to victory before a very long time had passed. (Applause). At this point an address from the Lancashire and Cheshire Conservative Working Men's Federation was presented to tho right hon. gentleman by Alderman Bennett (Warrington). AN ATTACK UN LABOUR REPRESENTATIVES. In acknowledging the address, Mr. Chamberlain said : —There is always a tendency in a nation to get small-i minded, to think of petty things. It has been a factc that we were the portion of a great empiie which has>. always maintained in this country a high tone of respon- sibility and national obligation. It delights me to find that in this address that feeling predominates. (Cheers). I believe that I have behind me the mass of the workin7 people. (Hear, hear.) We shall see. I do not mean at the next election. I mean whenever the issue is brought home clearly as the one great issue of an appeal ; then I believe that, with regal d to politics, the great mass of working people will be on my side. (Cheers). But oh! it is said Have you considered the representatives of labour are all against you ?" Who are the representa- tives of labour? (Loud cheers). Whom do you mean? Do you mean men who call themselves by that name, and who sit in Parliament with the assist- ance of a political party ? (Cheers.) Do you mean the men who are most excellent party politicians and who are very often very bad Labour repre- sentatives? Did these men represent you in the time of the Boer War. (" No ")? Everyman was against us, was against the war, and every man desired, or voted as if he desired that we should have surrendered and given up our position in the world and lost the confidence of the colonies, rather than continue the war. Did they represent you in regard to Home Rule. (" No ")? They represented some, no doubt. Did they represent you when the Compensation Act was Wore Parliament. ("No") ? Can any of you tell me of dny one of them who gave me any assistance? Did they represent you with regard to the Alien Bill. (" No ")? Well, what do they represent. (Laughter and cheers)? I appeal from them, if necessary—l don't think, after all, that they are so numerous as they themselves suppose—from them to those who made them; I appeal to the great democracy of this country who made them, who upon all those points to which I have referred were instinctively right, instinctively patriotic, instinctively prudent and wise. (Applause).

TRAINING COLLEGE CONCERT. .__. The Value of Musical Education. _ . Dr. Arthur Somervell, Board of Education Inspector of Training Colleges, visited the Warrington Training College on Wednesday and conducted the annual musical examination. As usual the collective test took the form of a concert in the evening, and, at the invitation of the Principal, there was a large gathering of promi" nent townspeople, including the Chairman (Alderman Bennett) and several members and officials of the Education Committee. The works selected for ff presentation by the second year students were Bach's "Sleepers, Wake I" and Matthews' "Song of the silent land." It is due to the students to say that the renderings of both compositions gave the greatest ! pleasure to the audience, and were obviously the result N of careful and painstaking training. The chorus was strengthened by the inclusion of local gentlemen who , supplied the tenor and bass parts. The soloists were ' Miss Annie Redman, Miss Eva Smith, and Mr. Bayard , Haddock who sang the various parts alloted to them with taste and expression. The duet "I seek Thee, m 1 Life" by Miss Redman and Mr. Haddock was partied' " larly effective. The second part of the programme wals • of a miscellaneous character and was submitted princi; pally by the first year students. "The song 04 k the birds " (Rnbinstein), "By Celia's Arbour., , (Mendelssohn) and "The passage-bird's farewell ,• ' (Mendelssohn), were given with delightful effect, and- the audience were very cordial in their expressions of, appreciation. Mr. E. Bayard Haddock was heard to dis: . tinct advantage in "0 ruddier than the cherrY', (Handel) and the item was one of the most enjoyable ot the evening. Valuable assistance was rendered by 113 . orchestra composed as follows :_Orchestra: Ist violin. Mr. R. H. Pearson; 2nd violin, Mr. E. H. Clare ; viola. Nlr. G. Johnson; violoncello, Mr. Henry Bond; double bass, Mr. J. Sudworth ; pianoforte, Miss Hackett: „ harmonium, Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac. ,;..,• , quartette for strings "Allegro moderato: Op. 1-';', (Schubert) was very creditably submitted as an open,...in& , item to the miscellaneous part of the programme. Dtr' W. S. Woods conducted throughout the evening with his customary ability. , • At the close Of the concert, the PRINCIPAL cordialll, • thanked the gentlemen who had assisted. He rernarkoa that even in a place which so much resembled the Garden of Eden as that college they had to acknowledge that they could not manage everything without the bele of the inferior sex. (Laughter.) Mr. Stevenson men; Moiled that although Mr. Matthews, the composer 01 i "The song of the silent hand," was unable to attend his father was present, having travelled from Cheltenhaci to near the work presented. Dr. SOMERVELL delivered an interesting speech 0° the value of music as an educational force. Music, h,et , said, was a recreation, and he thought we in Englal3l had hitherto been content to leave it at that. We tie' not yet realized that by placing it in that position were really neglecting one of the greatest force' , in education that we could possibly use. were only just beginning to find out the immense, ; part which was played in the education a child by what was called the sub-conscious rain ; Music appealed more to the sub-conscious mind o' children than anything else in the curriculum because there was no appeal to the intellect necessary at first' The intellectual enjoyment of music came later. Pr; Somervell pointed out how music was an expression ot the emotions, and in this connection strongly advisea the teaching of national songs to children. The teach:, ing of music, unlike other arts, did not require CO apparatus, and another advantage was that the chl!,' could sing a song practically perfectly the moment no knew it. Musical education in schools, it bad beet' found, improved discipline, awakened the imsginatioo9 "tamed ' children when out of school, benefited 'ciao! physically, and cultivated a sympathetic bond betwee' teachers and scholars.

THE EXAMINER. JUNE ICU 3 1905.

IThe great day draws nearer! Warrington Walking Day has been fixed for. Friday, June 30th, and the youngsters are already revelling in gleeful anticipation Of the festivities. There will be the usual processions and field sports, and not a few scholars will leave the town( for country resorts. * * 1 Among the amenities which were freely exchanged at Tuesday's lively Council meeting was the following "Your kindness is only exceeded by your brain power " Councillor Pemberton to -Councillor Wilkinson. This Was " kind'er sarcastic "but Coun- cillor Wilkinson looked distinctly flattered.

“His (Mr. Ernest's) mode of business justifies at least praise.”-- Glasgow Herald. Can We Tempt You

Before you buy or sell any kind of property or business in any part of the country, stop and think—it may save you hundreds of pounds. If you want to sell, you may be able to get the value of your property without any assistance, but the chances are that you will sell at a sacrifice. If you want to buy, you may be able to buy at a reasonable price without any kip, but it is more than probable that you will f!oay too much. If you could have your property submitted to a hundred possible buyers instead of only ten, you would be ten times as likely to get the value of your property. On the other hand, if you could have a hundred properties from which to select instead of only ten, you would be ten times as likely to get just what you want at a reasonable price. Now note the following facts and draw your own conclusions : We are spending £.10,000 a year in advertising for the purpose of finding buyers and sellers of every kind of property and business in every section of the country—and we rind them—in every town and county in the kingdom. We have offices in seven cities and a force of 500 agents and permanent employees. Our methods are endorsed by thousands of clients in all parts of the country. If you will send us your name and address on a post card, we will send you a booklet free containing too of these endorsements. Our business is recognised by Press, public, and competitors as the largest estate organisation in the world. Will you avail yourself of this service ? Will it be wise not to? This advertisement and many others are appearing in every journal of importance in the country. They bring us inquiries from buyers and sellers far and near—not in ones and twos, but in hundreds. Our organisation thus becomes the great clearing house for properties and businesses every- where. If you want to sell any kind of property or business—large or small—in town or country—North, South, East or West—send us brief particulars and price to-day, and we will outline (free of ,charge) a definite, practical plan for quickly converting it into cash.
